## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];

    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";

}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Writing Plans

## Overview

Write comprehensive implementation plans assuming the engineer has zero context for our codebase and questionable taste. Document everything they need to know: which files to touch for each task, code, testing, docs they might need to check, how to test it. Give them the whole plan as bite-sized tasks. DRY. YAGNI. TDD. Frequent commits.

Assume they are a skilled developer, but know almost nothing about our toolset or problem domain. Assume they don't know good test design very well.

**Announce at start:** "I'm using the writing-plans skill to create the implementation plan."

**Context:** This should be run in a dedicated worktree (created by brainstorming skill).

**Save plans to:** `docs/superpowers/plans/YYYY-MM-DD-<feature-name>.md`
- (User preferences for plan location override this default)

## Scope Check

If the spec covers multiple independent subsystems, it should have been broken into sub-project specs during brainstorming. If it wasn't, suggest breaking this into separate plans â€” one per subsystem. Each plan should produce working, testable software on its own.

## File Structure

Before defining tasks, map out which files will be created or modified and what each one is responsible for. This is where decomposition decisions get locked in.

- Design units with clear boundaries and well-defined interfaces. Each file should have one clear responsibility.
- You reason best about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. Prefer smaller, focused files over large ones that do too much.
- Files that change together should live together. Split by responsibility, not by technical layer.
- In existing codebases, follow established patterns. If the codebase uses large files, don't unilaterally restructure - but if a file you're modifying has grown unwieldy, including a split in the plan is reasonable.

This structure informs the task decomposition. Each task should produce self-contained changes that make sense independently.

## Bite-Sized Task Granularity

**Each step is one action (2-5 minutes):**
- "Write the failing test" - step
- "Run it to make sure it fails" - step
- "Implement the minimal code to make the test pass" - step
- "Run the tests and make sure they pass" - step
- "Commit" - step

## Plan Document Header

**Every plan MUST start with this header:**

```markdown
# [Feature Name] Implementation Plan

> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development (recommended) or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** [One sentence describing what this builds]

**Architecture:** [2-3 sentences about approach]

**Tech Stack:** [Key technologies/libraries]

---
```

## Task Structure

````markdown
### Task N: [Component Name]

**Files:**
- Create: `exact/path/to/file.py`
- Modify: `exact/path/to/existing.py:123-145`
- Test: `tests/exact/path/to/test.py`

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

```python
def test_specific_behavior():
    result = function(input)
    assert result == expected
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Run test to verify it fails**

Run: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`
Expected: FAIL with "function not defined"

- [ ] **Step 3: Write minimal implementation**

```python
def function(input):
    return expected
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Run test to verify it passes**

Run: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`
Expected: PASS

- [ ] **Step 5: Commit**

```bash
git add tests/path/test.py src/path/file.py
git commit -m "feat: add specific feature"
```
````

## No Placeholders

Every step must contain the actual content an engineer needs. These are **plan failures** â€” never write them:
- "TBD", "TODO", "implement later", "fill in details"
- "Add appropriate error handling" / "add validation" / "handle edge cases"
- "Write tests for the above" (without actual test code)
- "Similar to Task N" (repeat the code â€” the engineer may be reading tasks out of order)
- Steps that describe what to do without showing how (code blocks required for code steps)
- References to types, functions, or methods not defined in any task

## Remember
- Exact file paths always
- Complete code in every step â€” if a step changes code, show the code
- Exact commands with expected output
- DRY, YAGNI, TDD, frequent commits

## Self-Review

After writing the complete plan, look at the spec with fresh eyes and check the plan against it. This is a checklist you run yourself â€” not a subagent dispatch.

**1. Spec coverage:** Skim each section/requirement in the spec. Can you point to a task that implements it? List any gaps.

**2. Placeholder scan:** Search your plan for red flags â€” any of the patterns from the "No Placeholders" section above. Fix them.

**3. Type consistency:** Do the types, method signatures, and property names you used in later tasks match what you defined in earlier tasks? A function called `clearLayers()` in Task 3 but `clearFullLayers()` in Task 7 is a bug.

If you find issues, fix them inline. No need to re-review â€” just fix and move on. If you find a spec requirement with no task, add the task.

## Execution Handoff

After saving the plan, offer execution choice:

**"Plan complete and saved to `docs/superpowers/plans/<filename>.md`. Two execution options:**

**1. Subagent-Driven (recommended)** - I dispatch a fresh subagent per task, review between tasks, fast iteration

**2. Inline Execution** - Execute tasks in this session using executing-plans, batch execution with checkpoints

**Which approach?"**

**If Subagent-Driven chosen:**
- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development
- Fresh subagent per task + two-stage review

**If Inline Execution chosen:**
- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:executing-plans
- Batch execution with checkpoints for review

# Brainstorming Ideas Into Designs

Help turn ideas into fully formed designs and specs through natural collaborative dialogue.

Start by understanding the current project context, then ask questions one at a time to refine the idea. Once you understand what you're building, present the design and get user approval.

<HARD-GATE>
Do NOT invoke any implementation skill, write any code, scaffold any project, or take any implementation action until you have presented a design and the user has approved it. This applies to EVERY project regardless of perceived simplicity.
</HARD-GATE>

## Anti-Pattern: "This Is Too Simple To Need A Design"

Every project goes through this process. A todo list, a single-function utility, a config change â€” all of them. "Simple" projects are where unexamined assumptions cause the most wasted work. The design can be short (a few sentences for truly simple projects), but you MUST present it and get approval.

## Checklist

You MUST create a task for each of these items and complete them in order:

1. **Explore project context** â€” check files, docs, recent commits
2. **Offer visual companion** (if topic will involve visual questions) â€” this is its own message, not combined with a clarifying question. See the Visual Companion section below.
3. **Ask clarifying questions** â€” one at a time, understand purpose/constraints/success criteria
4. **Propose 2-3 approaches** â€” with trade-offs and your recommendation
5. **Present design** â€” in sections scaled to their complexity, get user approval after each section
6. **Write design doc** â€” save to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md` and commit
7. **Spec self-review** â€” quick inline check for placeholders, contradictions, ambiguity, scope (see below)
8. **User reviews written spec** â€” ask user to review the spec file before proceeding
9. **Transition to implementation** â€” invoke writing-plans skill to create implementation plan

## Process Flow

```dot
digraph brainstorming {
    "Explore project context" [shape=box];
    "Visual questions ahead?" [shape=diamond];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [shape=box];
    "Ask clarifying questions" [shape=box];
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" [shape=box];
    "Present design sections" [shape=box];
    "User approves design?" [shape=diamond];
    "Write design doc" [shape=box];
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" [shape=box];
    "User reviews spec?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke writing-plans skill" [shape=doublecircle];

    "Explore project context" -> "Visual questions ahead?";
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [label="yes"];
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Ask clarifying questions" [label="no"];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" -> "Ask clarifying questions";
    "Ask clarifying questions" -> "Propose 2-3 approaches";
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" -> "Present design sections";
    "Present design sections" -> "User approves design?";
    "User approves design?" -> "Present design sections" [label="no, revise"];
    "User approves design?" -> "Write design doc" [label="yes"];
    "Write design doc" -> "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)";
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" -> "User reviews spec?";
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Write design doc" [label="changes requested"];
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Invoke writing-plans skill" [label="approved"];
}
```

**The terminal state is invoking writing-plans.** Do NOT invoke frontend-design, mcp-builder, or any other implementation skill. The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.

## The Process

**Understanding the idea:**

- Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)
- Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., "build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics"), flag this immediately. Don't spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.
- If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec â†’ plan â†’ implementation cycle.
- For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea
- Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too
- Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions
- Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria

**Exploring approaches:**

- Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs
- Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning
- Lead with your recommended option and explain why

**Presenting the design:**

- Once you believe you understand what you're building, present the design
- Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced
- Ask after each section whether it looks right so far
- Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing
- Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn't make sense

**Design for isolation and clarity:**

- Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently
- For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?
- Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.
- Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that's often a signal that it's doing too much.

**Working in existing codebases:**

- Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.
- Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that's grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they're working in.
- Don't propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.

## After the Design

**Documentation:**

- Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`
  - (User preferences for spec location override this default)
- Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available
- Commit the design document to git

**Spec Self-Review:**
After writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:

1. **Placeholder scan:** Any "TBD", "TODO", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Fix them.
2. **Internal consistency:** Do any sections contradict each other? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?
3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?
4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? If so, pick one and make it explicit.

Fix any issues inline. No need to re-review â€” just fix and move on.

**User Review Gate:**
After the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:

> "Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Please review it and let me know if you want to make any changes before we start writing out the implementation plan."

Wait for the user's response. If they request changes, make them and re-run the spec review loop. Only proceed once the user approves.

**Implementation:**

- Invoke the writing-plans skill to create a detailed implementation plan
- Do NOT invoke any other skill. writing-plans is the next step.

## Key Principles

- **One question at a time** - Don't overwhelm with multiple questions
- **Multiple choice preferred** - Easier to answer than open-ended when possible
- **YAGNI ruthlessly** - Remove unnecessary features from all designs
- **Explore alternatives** - Always propose 2-3 approaches before settling
- **Incremental validation** - Present design, get approval before moving on
- **Be flexible** - Go back and clarify when something doesn't make sense

## Visual Companion

A browser-based companion for showing mockups, diagrams, and visual options during brainstorming. Available as a tool â€” not a mode. Accepting the companion means it's available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.

**Offering the companion:** When you anticipate that upcoming questions will involve visual content (mockups, layouts, diagrams), offer it once for consent:
> "Some of what we're working on might be easier to explain if I can show it to you in a web browser. I can put together mockups, diagrams, comparisons, and other visuals as we go. This feature is still new and can be token-intensive. Want to try it? (Requires opening a local URL)"

**This offer MUST be its own message.** Do not combine it with clarifying questions, context summaries, or any other content. The message should contain ONLY the offer above and nothing else. Wait for the user's response before continuing. If they decline, proceed with text-only brainstorming.

**Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**

- **Use the browser** for content that IS visual â€” mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs
- **Use the terminal** for content that is text â€” requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions

A question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. "What does personality mean in this context?" is a conceptual question â€” use the terminal. "Which wizard layout works better?" is a visual question â€” use the browser.

If they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:
`skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`

name: verification-before-completion
description: Use when about to claim work is complete, fixed, or passing, before committing or creating PRs - requires running verification commands and confirming output before making any success claims; evidence before assertions always

# Verification Before Completion

## Overview

Claiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.

**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.

**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE
```

If you haven't run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.

## The Gate Function

```
BEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:

1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?
2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)
3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures
4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?
   - If NO: State actual status with evidence
   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence
5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim

Skip any step = lying, not verifying
```

## Common Failures

| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |
|-------|----------|----------------|
| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, "should pass" |
| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |
| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |
| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |
| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |
| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports "success" |
| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |

## Red Flags - STOP

- Using "should", "probably", "seems to"
- Expressing satisfaction before verification ("Great!", "Perfect!", "Done!", etc.)
- About to commit/push/PR without verification
- Trusting agent success reports
- Relying on partial verification
- Thinking "just this once"
- Tired and wanting work over
- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**

## Rationalization Prevention

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Should work now" | RUN the verification |
| "I'm confident" | Confidence â‰  evidence |
| "Just this once" | No exceptions |
| "Linter passed" | Linter â‰  compiler |
| "Agent said success" | Verify independently |
| "I'm tired" | Exhaustion â‰  excuse |
| "Partial check is enough" | Partial proves nothing |
| "Different words so rule doesn't apply" | Spirit over letter |

## Key Patterns

**Tests:**
```
âœ… [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] "All tests pass"
âŒ "Should pass now" / "Looks correct"
```

**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**
```
âœ… Write â†’ Run (pass) â†’ Revert fix â†’ Run (MUST FAIL) â†’ Restore â†’ Run (pass)
âŒ "I've written a regression test" (without red-green verification)
```

**Build:**
```
âœ… [Run build] [See: exit 0] "Build passes"
âŒ "Linter passed" (linter doesn't check compilation)
```

**Requirements:**
```
âœ… Re-read plan â†’ Create checklist â†’ Verify each â†’ Report gaps or completion
âŒ "Tests pass, phase complete"
```

**Agent delegation:**
```
âœ… Agent reports success â†’ Check VCS diff â†’ Verify changes â†’ Report actual state
âŒ Trust agent report
```

## Why This Matters

From 24 failure memories:
- your human partner said "I don't believe you" - trust broken
- Undefined functions shipped - would crash
- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features
- Time wasted on false completion â†’ redirect â†’ rework
- Violates: "Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you'll be replaced."

## When To Apply

**ALWAYS before:**
- ANY variation of success/completion claims
- ANY expression of satisfaction
- ANY positive statement about work state
- Committing, PR creation, task completion
- Moving to next task
- Delegating to agents

**Rule applies to:**
- Exact phrases
- Paraphrases and synonyms
- Implications of success
- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness

## The Bottom Line

**No shortcuts for verification.**

Run the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.

This is non-negotiable.

.\env_map.tmp.txt:1:235: LEMMA [Finite rank of apparition] lem:finite-rank
.\env_map.tmp.txt:2:  \begin{lemma}[Finite rank of apparition]\label{lem:finite-rank} For every integer $q\ge1$, the rank of apparition $\alpha(q)$ is finite. More precisely, $\alpha(1)=1$, and for every $q\ge2$ there is a positive integer $r$ such that $q\mid F_r$. \end{lemma}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:5:  \begin{proposition}\label{prop:rank-basics} Let $q,u,v\ge1$ and let $a,b,m\ge1$. Then: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)] \item $q\mid F_m$ if and only if $\alpha(q)\mid m$. \item $\gcd(F_a,F_b)=F_{\gcd(a,b)}$. \item $\alpha(\lcmop(u,v))=\lcmop(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))$. \end{enumerate} \end{proposition}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:8:  \begin{theorem}\label{thm:primitive-divisors} For every $n\ge 3$ with $n\notin\{6,12\}$, there exists a prime $p_n$ with $\alpha(p_n)=n$. \end{theorem}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:11:  \begin{lemma}[Primitive-prime M\"obius count]\label{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count} For $d\ge1$, put \[ \Pi_\alpha(d):=\{p\text{ prime}:\alpha(p)=d\}. \] Then $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ is finite and \[ \#\Pi_\alpha(d)=\sum_{c\mid d}\mu(d/c)\,\omega(F_c). \] \end{lemma}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:14:  \begin{proposition}[Classical prime-power rank lifting] \label{prop:prime-power-ladders} Let $p$ be prime, and put $z_p:=\alpha(p)$. For $p\ne2,5$, put $h_p:=\nu_p(F_{z_p})$. Then the ranks of apparition of prime powers are as follows: \[ \alpha(2)=3,\qquad \alpha(4)=6,\qquad \alpha(2^e)=3\cdot2^{e-2}\quad(e\ge3), \] \[ \alpha(5^e)=5^e\quad(e\ge1), \] and, for $p\ne2,5$, \[ \alpha(p^e)=p^{\max\{e-h_p,0\}}\,z_p\quad(e\ge1). \] \end{proposition}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:17:  \begin{definition}\label{def:atomic-families} For a prime power $\theta=p^e$ with $e\ge1$, put \[ d(\theta):=\alpha(\theta),\qquad d^-(\theta):=\alpha(p^{e-1}), \] where $\alpha(1)=1$. For $d\ge1$ define \[ \Atom(d):=\{p^e:\ \alpha(p^e)=d,\ \alpha(p^{e-1})<d\}. \] Thus $\Atom(d)$ consists of the prime-power factors that are born exactly at rank $d$. If $d(\theta)\mid n$, define the full-coordinate support and the essential-coordinate support of $\theta$ relative to $n$ by \[ T_n(\theta):=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):\ \nu_\ell(d(\theta))=\nu_\ell(n)\}, \] and \[ E_n(\theta):=\{\ell\in T_n(\theta):\ \nu_\ell(d^-(\theta))<\nu_\ell(n)\}. \] Equivalently, $T_n(\theta)$ records the prime coordinates of $n$ supplied at full exponent by $d(\theta)$, while $E_n(\theta)$ records the full coordinates lost when $\theta=p^e$ is lowered to $p^{e-1}$. For nonempty $I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, put \[ \mathcal A_{I,J}(n):= \{\theta\in\bigcup_{d\mid n}\Atom(d):\ E_n(\theta)=I,\ T_n(\theta)=J\}. \] \end{definition}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:20:  \begin{corollary}\label{cor:atomic-classification} The atomic prime powers are exactly the following: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)] \item $2\in\Atom(3)$, $4\in\Atom(6)$, and $2^e\in\Atom(3\cdot2^{e-2})$ for every $e\ge4$; the power $8$ is not atomic. \item $5^e\in\Atom(5^e)$ for every $e\ge1$. \item If $p\ne2,5$, $z_p=\alpha(p)$, and $h_p=\nu_p(F_{z_p})$, then $p\in\Atom(z_p)$ and $p^e\in\Atom(p^{e-h_p}z_p)$ for every $e>h_p$; no power $p^e$ with $1<e\le h_p$ is atomic. \end{enumerate} Consequently every atom is either a primitive atom $p\in\Atom(\alpha(p))$ or a ladder atom $p^e$ with $e>1$. Every ladder atom changes only the $p$-adic coordinate of its apparition index when it is lowered to $p^{e-1}$. \end{corollary}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:23:  \begin{definition}\label{def:birth-layer} For $n\ge2$ let \[ D_n:=\operatorname{Div}(F_n),\qquad B_n:=\{q\in D_n:\ \alpha(q)=n\},\qquad A(n):=\#B_n. \] The set $B_n$ is partially ordered by divisibility. Its set of minimal elements is \[ \mathcal M_n:=\{m\in B_n:\ \text{there is no }q\in B_n \text{ with }q\mid m\text{ and }q<m\}, \] and \[ \widthop(B_n):=\max\{\#\mathcal A:\ \mathcal A\subseteq B_n \text{ is an antichain}\}. \] We write $\mathcal P(n)$ for the set of prime divisors of $n$. Since $F_2=1$, we use the boundary convention \[ B_2=\mathcal M_2=\varnothing,\qquad A(2)=\widthop(B_2)=0. \] \end{definition}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:29:  \begin{theorem}\label{thm:minimal-generators} Let $n\ge2$, and let $m=\prod_{i=1}^r p_i^{e_i}$ be a divisor of $F_n$, with the $p_i$ distinct and $e_i\ge1$. Put \[ \lambda_i:=\alpha(p_i^{e_i}),\qquad \lambda_i^-:=\alpha(p_i^{e_i-1}), \] where $\alpha(1)=1$. Then $m\in\mathcal M_n$ if and only if \begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-criterion} \lcmop(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r)=n \quad\text{and}\quad \lcmop(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{i-1},\lambda_i^-, \lambda_{i+1},\ldots,\lambda_r)<n \quad(1\le i\le r). \end{equation} \end{theorem}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:35:  \begin{lemma}[Coordinate test for lowering one atom] \label{lem:coordinate-lowering-test} Let $n\ge2$, and let $\Theta=\{\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_r\}$ be a pairwise coprime set of atomic prime powers with $d(\theta_i)\mid n$ and \[ \lcmop(d(\theta_1),\ldots,d(\theta_r))=n. \] Fix $\theta_i=p_i^{e_i}$, and put \[ N_i^-:=\lcmop\bigl(d^-(\theta_i),\ d(\theta_j):j\ne i\bigr). \] Then $N_i^-=n$ if and only if \[ E_n(\theta_i)\subseteq\bigcup_{j\ne i}T_n(\theta_j). \] Equivalently, lowering $\theta_i$ to $p_i^{e_i-1}$ keeps the apparition lcm equal to $n$ exactly when every coordinate lost by the lowering is supplied at full exponent by some other atom. If $\ell\in E_n(\theta_i)\setminus\bigcup_{j\ne i}T_n(\theta_j)$, then $\nu_\ell(N_i^-)<\nu_\ell(n)$, so $N_i^-<n$. \end{lemma}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:50:  \begin{corollary}[Best possible support budget] \label{cor:support-budget-best-possible} Let $g:\NN\to\NN$ be any function such that \[ \omega(m)\le g(\omega(n)) \] for every $n\ge2$ and every $m\in\mathcal M_n$. Then $g(k)\ge k$ for every $k\ge1$. Equivalently, Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} is sharp as a uniform bound depending only on the number of prime coordinates of $n$, not as a claim that equality occurs on every layer. \end{corollary}

.\env_map.tmp.txt:62:  \begin{corollary}\label{cor:boolean-skeleton} Let $n\ge3$, $n\notin\{6,12\}$, and put \[ \mathcal D_n^*:=\{d:\ d\mid n,\ 3\le d<n,\ d\notin\{6,12\}\}. \] For each $d\in\mathcal D_n^*$ choose a primitive prime $\pi_d$ with $\alpha(\pi_d)=d$, and choose a primitive prime $\pi_n$ with $\alpha(\pi_n)=n$. .\env_map.tmp.txt:110:  \begin{remark}\label{rem:wss} The only Wall--Sun--Sun input needed here is local. By Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders}, for $p\neq 2,5$ one has $\alpha(p^2)=\alpha(p)$ if and only if $\nu_p(F_{\alpha(p)})\ge 2$, the Wall--Sun--Sun condition for $p$ \cite{SunSun1992FibonacciFLT}. Thus the condition merely determines whether the first odd-prime ladder step is delayed; we do not use any distributional assertion about such primes. \end{remark}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:113:  \begin{definition}\label{def:support-hypergraph} Let $q=\prod_i p_i^{e_i}\in D_n$, and put $\lambda_i:=\alpha(p_i^{e_i})$. The \emph{support hypergraph} $H_n(q)$ has vertex set $\mathcal P(n)$ and one edge \[ \operatorname{supp}_n(\lambda_i):= \{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):\nu_\ell(\lambda_i)>0\} \] for each nonempty such support. We call $q$ \emph{connected} if $H_n(q)$ is connected on its vertex set. Let \[ B_n^{\mathrm{conn}}:=\{q\in B_n:\ H_n(q)\text{ is connected}\},\qquad A^{\mathrm{conn}}(n):=\#B_n^{\mathrm{conn}}, \] and \[ \mathcal M_n^{\mathrm{conn}}:= \{m\in\mathcal M_n:\ H_n(m)\text{ is connected}\},\qquad M^{\mathrm{conn}}(n):=\#\mathcal M_n^{\mathrm{conn}}. \] For a connected component $C$ of $H_n(q)$, define $q_C$ to be the product of those prime-power factors $p_i^{e_i}$ whose support edge lies in $C$. \end{definition}
.\env_map.tmp.txt:134:  \begin{corollary}\label{cor:odd-primitive-cover-realization} Let $n\ge3$ be odd. For each nonempty $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ put \[ n_S=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}. \] Choose a primitive prime $\pi_S$ with $\alpha(\pi_S)=n_S$, whose existence is guaranteed by Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors}. Then every set partition $\Pi$ of $\mathcal P(n)$ gives a minimal generator \[ m_\Pi:=\prod_{C\in\Pi}\pi_C\in\mathcal M_n. \] Thus \[ \#\mathcal M_n\ge \mathsf B_{\omega(n)}, \] and, since the minimal generators form an antichain in the divisor lattice, \[ \widthop(B_n)\ge \#\mathcal M_n\ge \mathsf B_{\omega(n)}, \] where $\mathsf B_k$ is the $k$th Bell number. \end{corollary}

Then $B_n$, $A(n)$, and $\mathcal M_n$ are computable by the following finite algorithm. \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)] \item Enumerate the divisor set $D_n=\operatorname{Div}(F_n)$ from the factorization of $F_n$. \item Enumerate the divisor set $\operatorname{Div}(n)$. For each $q\in D_n$, compute \[ \alpha_n(q):=\min\{d\mid n:q\mid F_d\}. \] \item Set $B_n=\{q\in D_n:q\ge2,\ \alpha_n(q)=n\}$ and $A(n)=\#B_n$. \item Sort $B_n$ by divisibility-compatible order, for instance by size, and retain exactly those $m\in B_n$ for which no previously retained element divides $m$; the retained set is $\mathcal M_n$. \end{enumerate} The operation count here is a high-level enumeration count after the integer factorization of $F_n$ is supplied: it counts divisibility tests $q\mid F_d$, divisor comparisons, and naive Fibonacci recurrence steps, but it is not a bit-complexity bound and includes neither the cost of factoring $F_n$ nor the bit sizes of the integers involved. .\scope_contract.md:10:        Establish the theorem chain of 'Upper fibers and witness covers for the Fibonacci order-of-apparition map'. For each positive integer $n$, let $B_n$ denote the set of divisors of the Fibonacci number $F_n$ whose rank of apparition equals~$n$. We prove that $B_n$ is an upper set in the divisor lattice of $F_n$ obtained by removing the covering ideals associated with the maximal proper divisors of~$n$. Our main result classifies the minimal generators of~$B_n$: every minimal generator factors uniquely as a pairwise coprime product of atomic prime-power birth moduli forming a witness cover whose support spans the prime coordinates of~$n$. This yields the bound $(m)(n)$ for every minimal generator $m$ and a primitive-versus-ladder dichotomy for atomic factors. We give complete classifications for $(n) 3$ and show that both $B_n$ and its minimal-generator set admit unique factorizations into connected coordinate blocks, producing partition-lattice formulas for $A(n) = \#B_n$ and $\#M_n$. As consequ

.\theorem_env_summary.tmp.txt:11:  [Finite rank of apparition] For every integer $q\ge1$, the rank of apparition $\alpha(q)$ is finite. More precisely, $\alpha(1)=1$, and for every $q\ge2$ there is a positive integer $r$ such that $q\mid F_r$.

.\theorem_env_summary.tmp.txt:44:  [Coordinate test for lowering one atom] Let $n\ge2$, and let $\Theta=\{\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_r\}$ be a pairwise coprime set of atomic prime powers with $d(\theta_i)\mid n$ and \lcmop(d(\theta_1),\ldots,d(\theta_r))=n. Equivalently, lowering $\theta_i$ to $p_i^{e_i-1}$ keeps the apparition lcm equal to $n$ exactly when every coordinate lost by the lowering is supplied at full exponent by some other atom. If $\ell\in E_n(\theta_i)\setminus\bigcup_{j\ne i}T_n(\theta_j)$, then $\nu_\ell(N_i^-)<\nu_\ell(n)$, so $N_i^-<n$.

.\theorem_extract.txt:29:The atomic prime powers are exactly the following: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)] \item $2\in\Atom(3)$, $4\in\Atom(6)$, and $2^e\in\Atom(3\cdot2^{e-2})$ for every $e\ge4$; the power $8$ is not atomic. \item $5^e\in\Atom(5^e)$ for every $e\ge1$. \item If $p\ne2,5$, $z_p=\alpha(p)$, and $h_p=\nu_p(F_{z_p})$, then $p\in\Atom(z_p)$ and $p^e\in\Atom(p^{e-h_p}z_p)$ for every $e>h_p$; no power $p^e$ with $1<e\le h_p$ is atomic. \end{enumerate} Consequently every atom is either a primitive atom $p\in\Atom(\alpha(p))$ or a ladder atom $p^e$ with $e>1$. Every ladder atom changes only the $p$-adic coordinate of its apparition index when it is lowered to $p^{e-1}$.

   45: \date{}

   47: \begin{document}
   48: \maketitle
   49: 
   50: \begin{abstract}
   51: For each positive integer $n$, let $B_n$ denote the set of divisors of the
   52: Fibonacci number $F_n$ whose rank of apparition under the map $\alpha$ equals
   53: $n$. We describe $B_n$ as an upper set in the divisor lattice of $F_n$ and
   54: classify its minimal elements by irredundant witness covers of the prime
   55: coordinates of $n$. The resulting support budget $\omega(m)\leq\omega(n)$ is
   56: best possible as a uniform bound. We construct a rank-pure exact-rank-prime
   57: sector that embeds every irredundant cover of the prime coordinates except for
   58: at most two explicitly identified exceptional supports, preserving the complete
   59: support hypergraph. If $k=\omega(n)$ and $R(n)$ is the largest number of
   60: atomic prime powers with one fixed essential/full support pair, then, uniformly
   61: as $k\to\infty$,
   62: \[
   63:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\geq
   64:  \frac{\log 2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k+O(1),
   65:  \qquad
   66:  \log\#\mathcal M_n
   67:  \leq \frac{\log 2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
   68: \]
   69: The lower bound has an explicit parity-dependent Jacobi theta constant and is
   70: realized by connected minimal generators. It does not imply that disconnected
   71: generators are negligible in the full arithmetic count. The upper estimate is
   72: not an asymptotic formula without additional control of the unresolved quantity
   73: $R(n)$. We prove that the mean contribution from aggregating several exact-rank
   74: classes in one support window is only $O(x\log\log x)$, reducing the mean
   75: interface to a visible single-rank maximum. We also show that a published
   76: conjecture of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek would contradict the resulting
   77: mean sparse-window hypothesis, and give a blockwise condition under which
   78: disconnected generators are exponentially negligible. Finally, we classify
   79: the cases $\omega(n)\leq3$ and prove unique connected-block factorizations for
   80: $B_n$ and its minimal elements.
   81: For the remaining single-rank maximum, a fibotomic argument gives the
   82: unconditional pointwise estimate
   83: $a(d)\leq(\log\varphi/2+o(1))\phi_{\rm E}(d)/\log d$, where
   84: $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ and $\phi_{\rm E}$ is Euler's function, together with
   85: new unconditional upper and lower bounds for its weighted mean contribution.
   86: \end{abstract}
   87: 
   88: \medskip
   89: \noindent\textbf{Keywords.} Fibonacci numbers, rank of apparition, divisor
   90: lattices, upper sets, witness covers, atomic decompositions.

   92: \smallskip
   93: \noindent\textbf{Mathematics Subject Classification (2020).}
   94: 11B39, 11A07, 11A25.
   95: 
  140: corresponding identity in the more general $(a,b)$-Fibonacci setting
  141: \cite{Renault2013PeriodRankOrder}. These properties, combined with the
  142: primitive divisor theorem for Fibonacci numbers
  143: \cite{Carmichael1913PrimitiveDivisors,BiluHanrotVoutier2001PrimitiveDivisorsLucas},
  144: are the sole inputs for the main results.
  145: 
  146: This point of view complements several nearby strands in the literature.
  147: Luca and Pomerance \cite{LucaPomerance2014LocalBehavior} study the local
  148: behavior of $\alpha$ under additive shifts; Trojovsk\'a
  149: \cite{Trojovska2020PeriodicPoints} and FitzGibbons--Javaheri--Miller--Verga
  150: \cite{FitzGibbonsJavaheriMillerVerga2025Dynamics} study periodic points and
  151: iteration dynamics of $\alpha$; Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong
  152: \cite{CameronCountsLundMillerPiechnikWong2022Fibotomic} study fibotomic
  153: polynomials and related algebraic factorizations; and Sanna
  154: \cite{Sanna2016LucasSequences} and Marques \cite{Marques2012OrderAppearance}
  155: study the growth and fixed-point behavior of the order-of-appearance map. The
  156: present paper instead fixes a single fiber $B_n$ and studies its divisibility
  157: geometry inside $D_n$. The upper-fiber identity, the coordinatewise minimal-lcm
  158: criterion, the prime--ladder alternative, the private-coordinate injection, and
  159: the coprimality of disjoint coordinate blocks are recorded below as lemmas:
  160: they are formal consequences of the cited classical identities and are not
  161: claimed as independent arithmetic advances. The contributions developed from
  162: these facts are the witness-cover formulation, the exhaustive support-three
  163: classification with its arithmetic realization test, the passage of connected
  164: block factorization to minimal elements, and the support-entropy coefficient
  165: $\log 2/4$. A further contribution is the support-preserving rank-pure
  166: embedding of arbitrary minimal set covers, which transfers the classical theta
  167: asymptotic and a discrete central local limit to an explicit Fibonacci sector.
  168: These lower results isolate, rather than resolve, the arithmetic multiplicity
  169: $R(n)$. Accordingly, we make no unconditional claim of a sharp asymptotic for
  170: $\#\mathcal M_n$ or of the negligibility of its disconnected part. We do,
  171: however, isolate the mean interface further: support-window aggregation costs
  172: only $O(x\log\log x)$ on average, so only a visible single-rank maximum remains.
  173: A fibotomic size estimate and the congruence restrictions on exact-rank primes
  174: then yield a uniform entropy inequality for each exact-rank class and a
  175: pointwise bound of order $\phi_{\rm E}(d)/\log d$. This improves the available
  176: single-rank input but does not settle the mean sparse-window hypothesis.
  177: A conditional dominance theorem also identifies the stronger blockwise control
  178: that would make the disconnected part exponentially negligible. The mean
  179: sparse-window hypothesis is not presented as a prediction: Conjecture~5.1 of
  180: Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
  181: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors} would imply its failure.
  182: 
  183: Call a prime power $\theta=p^e$ \emph{atomic for the index $d$} if
  184: $\alpha(\theta)=d$ and $\alpha(p^{e-1})<d$. An \emph{$n$-witness cover} is a
  185: pairwise coprime set of such atomic prime powers whose apparition indices
  186: collectively span the prime factorization of $n$ in a minimal, irredundant
  187: fashion; the full definition is Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}.
  188: 
  189: \begin{previewtheorem}[Witness-cover theorem]
  190: For every $n\ge2$, multiplication gives a bijection from $n$-witness
  191: covers $\Theta$ to minimal generators:
  192: \[
  193: \mathcal M_n=
  194: \left\{\prod_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta:\ \Theta\text{ is an $n$-witness cover}
  195: \right\}.
  196: \]
  197: Consequently every $m\in\mathcal M_n$ satisfies
  198: $\omega(m)\le\omega(n)$. This support budget is globally sharp in the precise
  199: sense that no smaller bound depending only on $\omega(n)$ is valid; equality
  200: is constructed on every odd layer $n\ge3$. The precise cover conditions are
  201: given in Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}, and the result is
  202: Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} together with
  203: Corollaries~\ref{cor:support-bound},~\ref{cor:support-bound-sharp},
  204: and~\ref{cor:support-budget-best-possible}.
  205: \end{previewtheorem}
  206: 
  207: The witness classification also controls the number of minimal generators,
  208: not merely their form.
  209: 
  210: \begin{previewtheorem}[Support-entropy theorem]
  211: For $n\ge3$, let $k=\omega(n)$ and let $R(n)$ be the largest atomic-family
  212: multiplicity at one essential/full support pair. If $k\ge3$ and
  213: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
  214: \[
  215:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}\le\#\mathcal M_n
  216:  \le\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
  217: \]
  218: The rank-pure sector gives the stronger connected lower bound
  219: \[
  220:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
  221:  \frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k+O(1)
  222: \]
  223: uniformly as $k\to\infty$, with an explicit parity-dependent theta constant.
  224: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is exact for the unweighted minimal-cover count
  225: embedded in the fixed-choice rank-pure sector. This assertion is not an
  226: optimality statement for all information obtainable from fibotomic sizes and
  227: exact-rank congruences. These lower bounds do not give an asymptotic formula
  228: for $\log\#\mathcal M_n$ unless $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$. Separate
  229: conjectures about $R(n)$ imply conditional normal-order and mean equivalents.
  230: These statements are
  231: Theorems~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} and
  232: \ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth}, together with their corollaries.
  233: \end{previewtheorem}

  235: Making this classification effective requires the prime-power apparition
 1510: $5$-coordinate $a_i$ exists only for $a_i\ge2$, and then it is $5^{a_i}$.
 1511: Finally suppose $\ell_i\notin\{2,5\}$. At atomic ladder levels,
 1512: \[
 1513: \alpha(\ell_i^e)=\ell_i^{e-h_i}z_i\qquad(e>h_i),
 1514: \]
 1515: so the full-coordinate equation
 1516: $\nu_{\ell_i}(\alpha(\ell_i^e))=a_i$ forces
 1517: $e=h_i+a_i-s_i$, and this is an atomic ladder exponent precisely when
 1518: $a_i>s_i$.
 1519: 
 1520: Thus every member of $L_{i,J}(n)$ lies in $\mathcal L_i^*(n)$. The remaining
 1521: conditions for membership in $L_{i,J}(n)$ are exactly that its rank divide
 1522: $n$ and that its full-coordinate set be $J$. Conversely, if
 1523: $\lambda\in\mathcal L_i^*(n)$ satisfies those two displayed conditions, the
 1524: same prime-power formulas show that $\lambda$ is an atomic ladder. Lowering
 1525: $\lambda$ reduces only the $\ell_i$-coordinate of its rank, so
 1526: $E_n(\lambda)=\{i\}$ and $T_n(\lambda)=J$. Hence
 1527: $\lambda\in L_{i,J}(n)$.
 1528: The displayed exact-rank windows and ladder candidate sets also exhaust the
 1529: exceptional cases: $2$ and $5$ are handled by their separate ladder lines,
 1530: the ranks $1,2,6,12$ are removed from exact-rank windows, and any
 1531: Wall--Sun--Sun plateau for $p\ne2,5$ is already absorbed in the parameter
 1532: $h_i=\nu_{\ell_i}(F_{z_i})$ before the single possible exponent
 1533: $h_i+a_i-s_i$ is formed.
 1534: \end{proof}
 1535: 
 1536: \begin{definition}[Three-coordinate slot construction]
 1537: \label{def:three-coordinate-slot-construction}
 1538: Assume $\mathcal P(n)=\{\ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_3\}$ and identify the prime
 1539: coordinates with $[3]=\{1,2,3\}$.  For every nonempty
 1540: $J\subseteq[3]$ let
 1541: \[
 1542: P_J(n)=\{\theta\in\mathcal A_{J,J}(n):\theta\text{ is a prime}\}
 1543: \]
 1544: be the exact-rank prime slot. For $i\in J$ let
 1545: \[
 1546: L_{i,J}(n)=\{\theta\in\mathcal A_{\{i\},J}(n):
 1547: \theta=p^e\text{ with }e>1\}
 1548: \]
 1549: be the ladder slot with essential coordinate $i$ and full support $J$, and put
 1550: \[
 1551: S_i(n):=P_{\{i\}}(n)\cup L_{i,\{i\}}(n).
 1552: \]
 1553: The following eight slot families, together with all coordinate permutations,
 1554: are called the admissible three-coordinate rows:
 1555: \[
 1556: \begin{array}{ccl}
 1557: \Gamma_1&:& P_{123},\\
 1558: \Gamma_3&:& L_{i,123},\\
 1559: \Gamma_4&:& P_{ij}P_{jk},\\
 1560: \Gamma_5&:& L_{i,ij}P_{jk},\\
 1561: \Gamma_6&:& L_{i,ij}L_{k,jk},\\
 1562: \Gamma_7&:& S_iP_{jk},\\
 1563: \Gamma_8&:& S_iL_{j,jk},\\
 1564: \Gamma_9&:& S_iS_jS_k,
 1565: \end{array}
 1566: \]
 1567: where $(i,j,k)$ ranges over ordered triples of distinct coordinates and braces
 1568: are suppressed in subscripts.  A \emph{labelled filling} of one of these rows
 1569: means choosing one prime power from each displayed slot after the coordinates
 1570: $1,2,3$ have been fixed; it is \emph{coprime} if the chosen prime powers have
 1571: distinct underlying primes.
 1572: \end{definition}
 1573: 
 1574: \begin{lemma}[Three-coordinate nonemptiness criterion]
 1575: \label{lem:three-coordinate-nonemptiness}
 1576: Assume $\mathcal P(n)=\{\ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_3\}$ and use the slots and rows of
 1577: Definition~\ref{def:three-coordinate-slot-construction}. Fix one labelled
 1578: coordinate permutation of one of the eight rows
 1579: \[
 1580: \Gamma_1,
 1581: \Gamma_3,
 1582: \Gamma_4,
 1583: \Gamma_5,
 1584: \Gamma_6,
 1585: \Gamma_7,
 1586: \Gamma_8,
 1587: \Gamma_9.
 1588: \]
 1589: Let $(I_j,J_j)$ be the essential and full coordinate supports attached to the
 1590: $j$th slot of the labelled row. The row is realized by an element of
 1591: $\mathcal M_n$ if and only if the following arithmetic selection test succeeds:
 1592: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
 1593: \item for every nonsingleton prime slot $I_j=J_j$, $\#J_j>1$, choose a prime
 1594: $\theta_j\in\Pi_\alpha(d_j)$ for some
 1595: $d_j\in\mathcal R_{J_j}^\circ(n)$;
 1596: \item for every proper ladder slot $I_j=\{i\}\subsetneq J_j$, choose the
 1597: unique candidate in $L_{i,J_j}(n)$ given by
 1598: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests};
 1599: \item for every singleton slot $I_j=J_j=\{i\}$, choose either an exact-rank
 1600: prime from $\bigcup_{d\in\mathcal R_{\{i\}}^\circ(n)}\Pi_\alpha(d)$ or the
 1601: unique ladder candidate in $L_{i,\{i\}}(n)$;
 1602: \item the chosen prime powers have distinct underlying primes.
 1603: \end{enumerate}
 1604: Equivalently, form for each slot its finite admissible set $C_j$: for a
 1605: prime slot use
 1606: $\bigcup_{d\in\mathcal R_{J_j}^\circ(n)}\Pi_\alpha(d)$, for a proper ladder
 1607: slot use $L_{i,J_j}(n)$, and for a singleton slot use
 1608: $P_{\{i\}}(n)\cup L_{i,\{i\}}(n)$.  The row succeeds precisely when these
 1609: sets satisfy the Hall inequalities of Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-slot-selection},
 1610: with underlying-prime multiplicities in each exact-rank window given by
 1611: $\#\Pi_\alpha(d)=\sum_{c\mid d}\mu(d/c)\omega(F_c)$.
 1612: 
 1613: In that case $m=\theta_1\cdots\theta_r$ is the realizing minimal generator.
 1614: Its labelled atomic factor set
 1615: \[
 1616:  \{(\theta,E_n(\theta),T_n(\theta)):\theta\text{ is a maximal prime-power
 1617:  factor of }m\}
 1618: \]
 1619: is unique. The associated unlabelled shape is unique only as the $S_3$-orbit
 1620: of the labelled support set $\mathsf T(m)$.
 1621: 
 1622: Equivalently, the rows in
 1623: Definition~\ref{def:three-coordinate-slot-construction} are unlabelled
 1624: representatives modulo coordinate relabeling: an unlabelled type $\Gamma_i$ is
 1625: realized if and only if the preceding labelled arithmetic selection test
 1626: succeeds for one of its coordinate permutations. The excluded type $\Gamma_2$
 1627: is never realized.
 1628: \end{lemma}
 1629: 
 1630: \begin{proof}
 1631: Suppose first that $m\in\mathcal M_n$ realizes the fixed labelled row. Write its witness cover as
 1632: $\Theta=\{\theta_1,\dots,\theta_r\}$ with the factors ordered so that
 1633: $(E_n(\theta_j),T_n(\theta_j))=(I_j,J_j)$. By definition
 1634: $\theta_j\in\mathcal A_{I_j,J_j}(n)$, and pairwise coprimeness is part of
 1635: Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}. Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-atom-families}
 1636: places each such factor in the corresponding prime or ladder family, and
 1637: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests} translates those
 1638: families into the displayed rank-window and ladder-candidate conditions. Thus
 1639: the arithmetic selection test succeeds.
 1640: 
 1641: Conversely, assume that the arithmetic selection test succeeds. By
 1642: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests}, each selected
 1643: prime power lies in the required prime or ladder family, and
 1644: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-atom-families} gives
 1645: $\theta_j\in\mathcal A_{I_j,J_j}(n)$. Distinct underlying primes are exactly
 1646: pairwise coprimeness for prime powers. Because the fixed row is an abstract
 1647: witness type, the sets $J_j$ cover $[3]$, and for every $j$ one has
 1648: $I_j\nsubseteq\bigcup_{h\ne j}J_h$. Translating back to $T_n$ and $E_n$, the
 1649: set $\Theta=\{\theta_1,\dots,\theta_r\}$ satisfies the coverage and
 1650: irredundance conditions in Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}; coprimeness
 1651: gives the remaining condition. Hence $\Theta$ is an $n$-witness cover, so
 1652: Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} gives
 1653: $m=\prod_j\theta_j\in\mathcal M_n$ with the prescribed row type.
 1654: 
 1655: The factorization of $m$ into maximal prime powers is unique, so it recovers
 1656: the displayed labelled atomic factor set uniquely. A raw enumeration over all
 1657: coordinate permutations may nevertheless repeat a labelled row or a product
 1658: when a $\Gamma_i$ has a nontrivial stabilizer. Accordingly, unlabelled shapes
 1659: are identified only at the level of $S_3$-orbits, and all enumerated products
 1660: are deduplicated. The final exclusion of $\Gamma_2$ is
 1661: Lemma~\ref{lem:fibonacci-three-coordinate-types}.
 1662: \end{proof}
 1663: 
 1664: \begin{lemma}[Three-coordinate realization]\label{lem:three-coordinate-realization}
 1665: Assume $\mathcal P(n)=\{\ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_3\}$. After the labelled coordinate
 1666: supports $E_n,T_n\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$ are fixed, every element of
 1667: $\mathcal M_n$ arises from exactly one nonempty arithmetic selection in the
 1668: rank windows $\mathcal R_J^\circ(n)$ and the explicit ladder candidates
 1669: $\mathcal L_i^*(n)$ belonging to one fixed labelled coordinate permutation of
 1670: one of the eight admissible types in
 3000: \]
 3001: because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By
 3002: Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
 3003: $E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
 3004: support-pair alternatives, and the definition
 3005: \eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
 3006: Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
 3007: $x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
 3008: coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
 3009: $\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
 3010: bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 3011: 
 3012: Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
 3013: $(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
 3014: $(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
 3015: $s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
 3016: $\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
 3017: \[
 3018:  \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 3019:  \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
 3020: \]
 3021: This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 3022: 
 3023: Let
 3024: \[
 3025:  \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
 3026: \]
 3027: This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
 3028: \[
 3029:  m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
 3030: \]
 3031: is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
 3032: Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
 3033: $k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
 3034: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3035: 
 3036: Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
 3037: $2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
 3038: induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
 3039: $\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
 3040: members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
 3041: $k$ with a new symbol; therefore
 3042: \[
 3043:  \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
 3044: \]
 3045: Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
 3046: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3047: 
 3048: The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
 3049: \cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
 3050: \[
 3051:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 3052:  \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
 3053: \]
 3054: now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
 3055: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
 3056: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
 3057: the other hand,
 3058: $\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
 3059: \[
 3060:  \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
 3061: \]
 3062: Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
 3063: \[
 3064:  \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
 3065: \]
 3066: Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
 3067: $\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
 3068: \eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
 3069: $(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
 3070: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.
 3071: 
 3072: For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
 3073: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
 3074: \begin{equation}
 3075: \label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 3076:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
 3077: \end{equation}
 3078: together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
 3079: $\log n\le\log x$, and hence
 3080: \[
 3081:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3082:  \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 3083:  =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 3084: \]
 3085: 
 3086: Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
 3087: \cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives
 3088: \begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 3089:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
 3090: \end{equation}
 3091: After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
 3092: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
 3093: $L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
 3094: Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
 3095: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
 3096: \end{proof}
 3097: 
 3098: \begin{lemma}[Known baseline for the arithmetic interface]
 3099: \label{lem:R-baseline}
 3100: For every $n\ge3$,
 3101: \[
 3102:  R(n)\le 1+\omega(F_n)=O(n),
 3103:  \qquad
 3104:  \log R(n)\le\log n+O(1).
 3105: \]
 3106: Moreover, Stroi\'nski's cumulative estimate gives
 3107: \[
 3108:  \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 3109:  \#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\}
 3110:  \le \frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
 3111: \]
 3112: Neither estimate implies the conjectures below.
 3113: \end{lemma}
 3114: 
 3115: \begin{proof}
 3116: Every prime atom in a family $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ divides $F_n$, and a fixed
 3117: ladder family contains at most one atom. Thus a family contains at most all
 3118: distinct prime divisors of $F_n$ together with one ladder atom, proving
 3119: $R(n)\le1+\omega(F_n)$. Since
 3120: $2^{\omega(F_n)}\le\operatorname{rad}(F_n)\le F_n$ and
 3121: $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$, this gives $R(n)=O(n)$ and the logarithmic
 3122: bound. The cumulative estimate is Stroi\'nski's Theorem~7
 3123: \cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction}.
 3124: 
 3125: Quantitatively, the normal-order conjecture below asks for
 3126: $\log R(n)=o(\log\log n)$ for almost all $n$, whereas the elementary estimate
 3127: gives only $O(\log n)$. The cumulative estimate controls all primes of rank at
 3128: most $x$ together and likewise gives at best a logarithmic bound of order
 3129: $\log n$ for a single window. For the mean conjecture, the elementary bound
 3130: and the mean order of $\omega$ give only
 3131: $O(x\log x\log\log x)$, not $o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 3132: \end{proof}
 3133: 
 3134: \begin{remark}[Cumulative rank estimates do not imply \textup{(H2)}]
 3135: \label{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient}
 3136: This nonimplication can be made quantitative without asserting that the model
 3137: below is realized by Fibonacci ranks. Fix $0<c<\log\varphi/2$ and put
 3138: \[
 3139:  a_0(d):=\max\left\{1,
 3140:  \left\lfloor\frac{c\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)}\right\rfloor\right\}.
 3141: \]
 3142: Elementary integral comparison gives
 3143: \[
 3144:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\ll\frac{y^2}{\log y},\qquad
 3145:  a_0(d)\ll1+\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)},\qquad
 3146:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\log d\ll y^2.
 3147: \]
 3148: Thus this formal multiplicity sequence has the cumulative, pointwise, and
 3149: logarithmic-mass scales supplied by the presently known estimates, including
 3150: Theorem~\ref{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. It is not asserted to arise from
 3151: Fibonacci exact-rank primes, and therefore it neither proves sharpness of the
 3152: fibotomic inequality nor supplies a realizable extremal model for it. On the
 3153: other hand, the standard
 3154: lower bound for Euler's function gives
 3155: \[
 3156:  \log a_0(n)=\log n-\log\log n+O(\log\log\log(3n))
 3157: \]
 3158: for large $n$.
 3159: Partial summation in \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input} therefore gives
 3160: \[
 3161:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log a_0(n)
 3162:  \sim x\log x\log\log x,
 3163: \]
 3164: which is larger than the scale in \textup{(H2)}. Hence cumulative exact-rank
 3165: counts alone cannot establish the required visible-divisor maximum estimate.
 3166: \end{remark}
 3167: 
 3168: \begin{corollary}[Unconditional lower growth]
 3169: \label{cor:unconditional-lower-growth}
 3170: For almost all $n$,
 3171: \[
 3172:  \log\#\mathcal M_n\ge
 3173:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)(\log\log n)^2.
 3174: \]
 3175: If $N_y:=\prod_{p\le y}p$, then
 3176: \[
 3177:  \log\#\mathcal M_{N_y}\ge
 3178:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)
 3179:  \frac{(\log N_y)^2}{(\log\log N_y)^2}.
 3180: \]
 3181: \end{corollary}
 3182: 
 3183: \begin{proof}
 3184: The Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order theorem states that
 3185: $\omega(n)=(1+o(1))\log\log n$ for almost all $n$
 3186: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors,%
 3187: Tenenbaum2015AnalyticProbabilistic}. Substitution in the lower half of
 3188: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} proves the first assertion. The prime
 3189: number theorem gives
 3190: $\omega(N_y)=\pi(y)\sim y/\log y\sim\log N_y/\log\log N_y$; substituting this
 3191: in the same lower bound proves the second assertion.
 3192: \end{proof}
 3193: 
 3194: \begin{conjecture}[Normal rank-window sparsity]\label{hyp:normal-window}
 3195: For every $\varepsilon>0$,
 3196: \[
 3197:  \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
 3198: \tag{H1}
 3199: \]
 3200: \end{conjecture}
 3201: 
 3202: \begin{conjecture}[Mean sparse-window hypothesis]\label{hyp:mean-window}
 3203: One has
 3204: \[
 3205:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 3206:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 3207: \tag{H2}
 3208: \]
 3209: \end{conjecture}
 3210: 
 3211: \begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
 3212: \label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
 3213: Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
 3214: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
 3215: $\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then
 3216: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 3217:  \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 3218:  \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 3219:  {x(\log\log x)^2}
 3220:  \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
 3221: \end{equation}
 3222: In particular, under that published conjecture, the mean sparse-window
 3223: hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is false.
 3224: \end{proposition}
 3225: 
 3226: \begin{proof}
 3227: Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
 3228: prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
 3229: \[
 3230:  \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
 3231: \]
 3232: For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
 3233: single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
 3234: pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
 3235: \begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 3236:  R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 3237:  \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
 3238: \end{equation}
 3239: Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
 3240: $n$,
 3241: \[
 3242:  \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
 3243: \]
 3244: The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
 3245: all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
 3246: $k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
 3247: \[
 3248:  \omega(n)\log R(n)
 3249:  \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
 3250: \]

本轮核验确认：关于可见单秩最大值
\[
A^*(n)=\max\left\{1,\max_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)\ne\varnothing}}\#\Pi_\alpha(d)\right\},
\qquad
S(x)=\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
\]
的命题
\[
S(x)=o\!\left(x(\log\log x)^2\right)
\]
仍未得到无条件证明，其临界尺度反向下界亦未得到无条件证明。该未决性及其与平均秩窗稀疏假设的等价接口已经写入本文；不得再次将其表述为新结论，亦不得以启发式、数值样本或条件命题替代无条件承重定理。

文献边界必须作如下校正。Granville 2012 的奇重数定理适用于满足 $c\equiv2\pmod4$ 的一类二阶 Lucas 递推；原文明确指出其方法没有覆盖 $b,c$ 同为奇数的情形，因而不适用于 Fibonacci 递推 $b=c=1$。Hong 2025 证明的是：对每个固定正整数 \(\kappa\)，充分大的 \(n\) 所对应的 \(F_n\) 存在一个不小于 \((\kappa+1)n-1\) 的 primitive divisor；该结论不控制精确秩素数的平均分散或临界集中。Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek 2005 的 \(\omega(F_n)\gg\log n\) 仅为其 Conjecture 5.1；本文已经证明该猜想将推出平均稀疏假设失败的条件性障碍。Stroiński 的累计精确秩素数上界、本文的逐秩 fibotomic 熵不等式及其点态 Euler 因子上界也均已出现。请避开这些已经发表或已在本文证明的论域。

此前关于“fibotomic 熵已经穷尽 primitive part 大小与秩同余所含信息”的最优性断言没有证明。缺失之处是：尚未定义允许使用的信息公理类，也没有构造满足全部公理且达到相应上界的可实现极值模型。本文现仅断言 fibotomic 大小恒等式、精确秩 radical 的整除关系及 $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$ 共同推出既有熵不等式；不得恢复信息穷尽或普适最优性主张，除非同时给出精确公理类与可实现极值模型的完整证明。

下一轮只研究下述单一问题：无条件确定 $S(x)$ 在临界尺度 $x(\log\log x)^2$ 上的真实数量级。必须证明 $S(x)=o(x(\log\log x)^2)$，或证明存在绝对常数 $c>0$ 使
\[
\liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac{S(x)}{x(\log\log x)^2}\ge c,
\]
或给出严格证明表明二者均不成立并确定正确尺度。答案必须包含一个此前未发表、可独立核验的平均分散或临界集中定理，直接作用于可见除数窗中的 \(\#\Pi_\alpha(d)\)；必须逐步证明该定理并明确说明其如何闭合上述三择一判定。不得仅重述 primitive divisor 的存在、单个因子的大小、累计计数上界、逐点 fibotomic 上界或 BLMS 条件推论。

   45: \date{}

   47: \begin{document}
   48: \maketitle
   49: 
   50: \begin{abstract}
   51: For each positive integer $n$, let $B_n$ denote the set of divisors of the
   52: Fibonacci number $F_n$ whose rank of apparition under the map $\alpha$ equals
   53: $n$. We describe $B_n$ as an upper set in the divisor lattice of $F_n$ and
   54: classify its minimal elements by irredundant witness covers of the prime
   55: coordinates of $n$. The resulting support budget $\omega(m)\leq\omega(n)$ is
   56: best possible as a uniform bound. We construct a rank-pure exact-rank-prime
   57: sector that embeds every irredundant cover of the prime coordinates except for
   58: at most two explicitly identified exceptional supports, preserving the complete
   59: support hypergraph. If $k=\omega(n)$ and $R(n)$ is the largest number of
   60: atomic prime powers with one fixed essential/full support pair, then, uniformly
   61: as $k\to\infty$,
   62: \[
   63:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\geq
   64:  \frac{\log 2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k+O(1),
   65:  \qquad
   66:  \log\#\mathcal M_n
   67:  \leq \frac{\log 2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
   68: \]
   69: The lower bound has an explicit parity-dependent Jacobi theta constant and is
   70: realized by connected minimal generators. It does not imply that disconnected
   71: generators are negligible in the full arithmetic count. The upper estimate is
   72: not an asymptotic formula without additional control of the unresolved quantity
   73: $R(n)$. We prove that the mean contribution from aggregating several exact-rank
   74: classes in one support window is only $O(x\log\log x)$, reducing the mean
   75: interface to a visible single-rank maximum. We also show that a published
   76: conjecture of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek would contradict the resulting
   77: mean sparse-window hypothesis, and give a blockwise condition under which
   78: disconnected generators are exponentially negligible. Finally, we classify
   79: the cases $\omega(n)\leq3$ and prove unique connected-block factorizations for
   80: $B_n$ and its minimal elements.
   81: For the remaining single-rank maximum, a fibotomic argument gives the
   82: unconditional pointwise estimate
   83: $a(d)\leq(\log\varphi/2+o(1))\phi_{\rm E}(d)/\log d$, where
   84: $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ and $\phi_{\rm E}$ is Euler's function, together with
   85: new unconditional upper and lower bounds for its weighted mean contribution.
   86: \end{abstract}
   87: 
   88: \medskip
   89: \noindent\textbf{Keywords.} Fibonacci numbers, rank of apparition, divisor
   90: lattices, upper sets, witness covers, atomic decompositions.
   91: 
   92: \smallskip
   93: \noindent\textbf{Mathematics Subject Classification (2020).}
   94: 11B39, 11A07, 11A25.
   95: 
  140: corresponding identity in the more general $(a,b)$-Fibonacci setting
  141: \cite{Renault2013PeriodRankOrder}. These properties, combined with the
  142: primitive divisor theorem for Fibonacci numbers
  143: \cite{Carmichael1913PrimitiveDivisors,BiluHanrotVoutier2001PrimitiveDivisorsLucas},
  144: are the sole inputs for the main results.
