  146: This point of view complements several nearby strands in the literature.
  147: Luca and Pomerance \cite{LucaPomerance2014LocalBehavior} study the local
  148: behavior of $\alpha$ under additive shifts; Trojovsk\'a
  149: \cite{Trojovska2020PeriodicPoints} and FitzGibbons--Javaheri--Miller--Verga
  150: \cite{FitzGibbonsJavaheriMillerVerga2025Dynamics} study periodic points and
  151: iteration dynamics of $\alpha$; Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong
  152: \cite{CameronCountsLundMillerPiechnikWong2022Fibotomic} study fibotomic
  153: polynomials and related algebraic factorizations; and Sanna
  154: \cite{Sanna2016LucasSequences} and Marques \cite{Marques2012OrderAppearance}
  155: study the growth and fixed-point behavior of the order-of-appearance map. The
  156: present paper instead fixes a single fiber $B_n$ and studies its divisibility
  157: geometry inside $D_n$. The upper-fiber identity, the coordinatewise minimal-lcm
  158: criterion, the prime--ladder alternative, the private-coordinate injection, and
  159: the coprimality of disjoint coordinate blocks are recorded below as lemmas:
  160: they are formal consequences of the cited classical identities and are not
  161: claimed as independent arithmetic advances. The contributions developed from
  162: these facts are the witness-cover formulation, the exhaustive support-three
  163: classification with its arithmetic realization test, the passage of connected
  164: block factorization to minimal elements, and the support-entropy coefficient
  165: $\log 2/4$. A further contribution is the support-preserving rank-pure
  166: embedding of arbitrary minimal set covers, which transfers the classical theta
  167: asymptotic and a discrete central local limit to an explicit Fibonacci sector.
  168: These lower results isolate, rather than resolve, the arithmetic multiplicity
  169: $R(n)$. Accordingly, we make no unconditional claim of a sharp asymptotic for
  170: $\#\mathcal M_n$ or of the negligibility of its disconnected part. We do,
  171: however, isolate the mean interface further: support-window aggregation costs
  172: only $O(x\log\log x)$ on average, so only a visible single-rank maximum remains.
  173: A fibotomic size estimate and the congruence restrictions on exact-rank primes
  174: then yield a uniform entropy inequality for each exact-rank class and a
  175: pointwise bound of order $\phi_{\rm E}(d)/\log d$. This improves the available
  176: single-rank input but does not settle the mean sparse-window hypothesis.
  177: A conditional dominance theorem also identifies the stronger blockwise control
  178: that would make the disconnected part exponentially negligible. The mean
  179: sparse-window hypothesis is not presented as a prediction: Conjecture~5.1 of
  180: Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
  181: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors} would imply its failure.
  182: 
  183: Call a prime power $\theta=p^e$ \emph{atomic for the index $d$} if
  184: $\alpha(\theta)=d$ and $\alpha(p^{e-1})<d$. An \emph{$n$-witness cover} is a
  185: pairwise coprime set of such atomic prime powers whose apparition indices
  186: collectively span the prime factorization of $n$ in a minimal, irredundant
  187: fashion; the full definition is Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}.
  188: 
  189: \begin{previewtheorem}[Witness-cover theorem]
  190: For every $n\ge2$, multiplication gives a bijection from $n$-witness
  191: covers $\Theta$ to minimal generators:
  192: \[
  193: \mathcal M_n=
  194: \left\{\prod_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta:\ \Theta\text{ is an $n$-witness cover}
  195: \right\}.
  196: \]
  197: Consequently every $m\in\mathcal M_n$ satisfies
  198: $\omega(m)\le\omega(n)$. This support budget is globally sharp in the precise
  199: sense that no smaller bound depending only on $\omega(n)$ is valid; equality
  200: is constructed on every odd layer $n\ge3$. The precise cover conditions are
  201: given in Definition~\ref{def:witness-cover}, and the result is
  202: Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} together with
  203: Corollaries~\ref{cor:support-bound},~\ref{cor:support-bound-sharp},
  204: and~\ref{cor:support-budget-best-possible}.
  205: \end{previewtheorem}
  206: 
  207: The witness classification also controls the number of minimal generators,
  208: not merely their form.
  209: 
  210: \begin{previewtheorem}[Support-entropy theorem]
  211: For $n\ge3$, let $k=\omega(n)$ and let $R(n)$ be the largest atomic-family
  212: multiplicity at one essential/full support pair. If $k\ge3$ and
  213: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
  214: \[
  215:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}\le\#\mathcal M_n
  216:  \le\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
  217: \]
  218: The rank-pure sector gives the stronger connected lower bound
  219: \[
  220:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
  221:  \frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k+O(1)
  222: \]
  223: uniformly as $k\to\infty$, with an explicit parity-dependent theta constant.
  224: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is exact for the unweighted minimal-cover count
  225: embedded in the fixed-choice rank-pure sector. This assertion is not an
  226: optimality statement for all information obtainable from fibotomic sizes and
  227: exact-rank congruences. These lower bounds do not give an asymptotic formula
  228: for $\log\#\mathcal M_n$ unless $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$. Separate
  229: conjectures about $R(n)$ imply conditional normal-order and mean equivalents.
  230: These statements are
  231: Theorems~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} and
  232: \ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth}, together with their corollaries.
  233: \end{previewtheorem}
  234: 
  235: Making this classification effective requires the prime-power apparition
 3000: \]
 3001: because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By
 3002: Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
 3003: $E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
 3004: support-pair alternatives, and the definition
 3005: \eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
 3006: Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
 3007: $x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
 3008: coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
 3009: $\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
 3010: bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 3011: 
 3012: Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
 3013: $(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
 3014: $(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
 3015: $s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
 3016: $\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
 3017: \[
 3018:  \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 3019:  \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
 3020: \]
 3021: This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 3022: 
 3023: Let
 3024: \[
 3025:  \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
 3026: \]
 3027: This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
 3028: \[
 3029:  m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
 3030: \]
 3031: is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
 3032: Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
 3033: $k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
 3034: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3035: 
 3036: Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
 3037: $2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
 3038: induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
 3039: $\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
 3040: members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
 3041: $k$ with a new symbol; therefore
 3042: \[
 3043:  \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
 3044: \]
 3045: Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
 3046: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3047: 
 3048: The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
 3049: \cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
 3050: \[
 3051:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 3052:  \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
 3053: \]
 3054: now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
 3055: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
 3056: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
 3057: the other hand,
 3058: $\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
 3059: \[
 3060:  \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
 3061: \]
 3062: Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
 3063: \[
 3064:  \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
 3065: \]
 3066: Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
 3067: $\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
 3068: \eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
 3069: $(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
 3070: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.
 3071: 
 3072: For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
 3073: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
 3074: \begin{equation}
 3075: \label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 3076:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
 3077: \end{equation}
 3078: together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
 3079: $\log n\le\log x$, and hence
 3080: \[
 3081:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3082:  \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 3083:  =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 3084: \]
 3085: 
 3086: Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
 3087: \cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives
 3088: \begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 3089:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
 3090: \end{equation}
 3091: After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
 3092: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
 3093: $L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
 3094: Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
 3095: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
 3096: \end{proof}
 3097: 
 3098: \begin{lemma}[Known baseline for the arithmetic interface]
 3099: \label{lem:R-baseline}
 3100: For every $n\ge3$,
 3101: \[
 3102:  R(n)\le 1+\omega(F_n)=O(n),
 3103:  \qquad
 3104:  \log R(n)\le\log n+O(1).
 3105: \]
 3106: Moreover, Stroi\'nski's cumulative estimate gives
 3107: \[
 3108:  \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 3109:  \#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\}
 3110:  \le \frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
 3111: \]
 3112: Neither estimate implies the conjectures below.
 3113: \end{lemma}
 3114: 
 3115: \begin{proof}
 3116: Every prime atom in a family $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ divides $F_n$, and a fixed
 3117: ladder family contains at most one atom. Thus a family contains at most all
 3118: distinct prime divisors of $F_n$ together with one ladder atom, proving
 3119: $R(n)\le1+\omega(F_n)$. Since
 3120: $2^{\omega(F_n)}\le\operatorname{rad}(F_n)\le F_n$ and
 3121: $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$, this gives $R(n)=O(n)$ and the logarithmic
 3122: bound. The cumulative estimate is Stroi\'nski's Theorem~7
 3123: \cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction}.
 3124: 
 3125: Quantitatively, the normal-order conjecture below asks for
 3126: $\log R(n)=o(\log\log n)$ for almost all $n$, whereas the elementary estimate
 3127: gives only $O(\log n)$. The cumulative estimate controls all primes of rank at
 3128: most $x$ together and likewise gives at best a logarithmic bound of order
 3129: $\log n$ for a single window. For the mean conjecture, the elementary bound
 3130: and the mean order of $\omega$ give only
 3131: $O(x\log x\log\log x)$, not $o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 3132: \end{proof}
 3133: 
 3134: \begin{remark}[Cumulative rank estimates do not imply \textup{(H2)}]
 3135: \label{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient}
 3136: This nonimplication can be made quantitative without asserting that the model
 3137: below is realized by Fibonacci ranks. Fix $0<c<\log\varphi/2$ and put
 3138: \[
 3139:  a_0(d):=\max\left\{1,
 3140:  \left\lfloor\frac{c\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)}\right\rfloor\right\}.
 3141: \]
 3142: Elementary integral comparison gives
 3143: \[
 3144:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\ll\frac{y^2}{\log y},\qquad
 3145:  a_0(d)\ll1+\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)},\qquad
 3146:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\log d\ll y^2.
 3147: \]
 3148: Thus this formal multiplicity sequence has the cumulative, pointwise, and
 3149: logarithmic-mass scales supplied by the presently known estimates, including
 3150: Theorem~\ref{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. It is not asserted to arise from
 3151: Fibonacci exact-rank primes, and therefore it neither proves sharpness of the
 3152: fibotomic inequality nor supplies a realizable extremal model for it. On the
 3153: other hand, the standard
 3154: lower bound for Euler's function gives
 3155: \[
 3156:  \log a_0(n)=\log n-\log\log n+O(\log\log\log(3n))
 3157: \]
 3158: for large $n$.
 3159: Partial summation in \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input} therefore gives
 3160: \[
 3161:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log a_0(n)
 3162:  \sim x\log x\log\log x,
 3163: \]
 3164: which is larger than the scale in \textup{(H2)}. Hence cumulative exact-rank
 3165: counts alone cannot establish the required visible-divisor maximum estimate.
 3166: \end{remark}
 3167: 
 3168: \begin{corollary}[Unconditional lower growth]
 3169: \label{cor:unconditional-lower-growth}
 3170: For almost all $n$,
 3171: \[
 3172:  \log\#\mathcal M_n\ge
 3173:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)(\log\log n)^2.
 3174: \]
 3175: If $N_y:=\prod_{p\le y}p$, then
 3176: \[
 3177:  \log\#\mathcal M_{N_y}\ge
 3178:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)
 3179:  \frac{(\log N_y)^2}{(\log\log N_y)^2}.
 3180: \]
 3181: \end{corollary}
 3182: 
 3183: \begin{proof}
 3184: The Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order theorem states that
 3185: $\omega(n)=(1+o(1))\log\log n$ for almost all $n$
 3186: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors,%
 3187: Tenenbaum2015AnalyticProbabilistic}. Substitution in the lower half of
 3188: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} proves the first assertion. The prime
 3189: number theorem gives
 3190: $\omega(N_y)=\pi(y)\sim y/\log y\sim\log N_y/\log\log N_y$; substituting this
 3191: in the same lower bound proves the second assertion.
 3192: \end{proof}
 3193: 
 3194: \begin{conjecture}[Normal rank-window sparsity]\label{hyp:normal-window}
 3195: For every $\varepsilon>0$,
 3196: \[
 3197:  \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
 3198: \tag{H1}
 3199: \]
 3200: \end{conjecture}
 3201: 
 3202: \begin{conjecture}[Mean sparse-window hypothesis]\label{hyp:mean-window}
 3203: One has
 3204: \[
 3205:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 3206:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 3207: \tag{H2}
 3208: \]
 3209: \end{conjecture}
 3210: 
 3211: \begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
 3212: \label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
 3213: Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
 3214: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
 3215: $\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then
 3216: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 3217:  \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 3218:  \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 3219:  {x(\log\log x)^2}
 3220:  \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
 3221: \end{equation}
 3222: In particular, under that published conjecture, the mean sparse-window
 3223: hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is false.
 3224: \end{proposition}
 3225: 
 3226: \begin{proof}
 3227: Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
 3228: prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
 3229: \[
 3230:  \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
 3231: \]
 3232: For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
 3233: single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
 3234: pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
 3235: \begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 3236:  R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 3237:  \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
 3238: \end{equation}
 3239: Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
 3240: $n$,
 3241: \[
 3242:  \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
 3243: \]
 3244: The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
 3245: all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
 3246: $k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
 3247: \[
 3248:  \omega(n)\log R(n)
 3249:  \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
 3250: \]
 3550: Hence the test in step (c) gives precisely the definition
 3551: $B_n=\{q\in D_n:\alpha(q)=n\}$, with the harmless exclusion of $q=1$ already
 3552: consistent with the convention $\alpha(1)=1$ and $n\ge2$. Counting this set
 3553: gives $A(n)$.
 3554: 
 3555: It remains to justify step (d). The set $B_n$ is finite. If its elements are
 3556: processed in increasing size, then every proper divisor of the current element
 3557: has already been processed. We claim inductively that every discarded element
 3558: has a retained minimal divisor. The claim is vacuous before the first element.
 3559: If the current element is discarded, it has a previously retained divisor by
 3560: the algorithm, so the claim holds for it; if it is retained, it is its own
 3561: retained divisor. Thus every earlier divisor, retained or discarded, has a
 3562: retained divisor below it. Consequently the current element is retained if and
 3563: only if no smaller element of $B_n$ divides it, which is exactly minimality in
 3564: the divisibility poset. The retained set is therefore $\mathcal M_n$.
 3565: \end{proof}
 3566: 
 3567: \begin{remark}[Reproducibility scope]
 3568: The preceding proposition is used only to document the finite verification
 3569: interface for the table, not as a complexity theorem.  After the factorization
 3570: of $F_n$ has been supplied, the displayed loops involve only the finite divisor
 3571: sets of $F_n$ and $n$, ordinary divisibility tests $q\mid F_d$, and divisor
 3572: comparisons inside $B_n$.  The manuscript makes no bit-complexity assertion and
 3573: does not include the cost of factoring $F_n$ in the numerical evidence.
 3574: \end{remark}
 3575: 
 3576: \paragraph{Table generation.}
 3577: The reproducibility files
 3578: \[
 3579: \text{\texttt{artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}},\qquad
 3580: \text{\texttt{artifacts/tab\_birth\_layer\_data.tex}}
 3581: \]
 3582: are included with the manuscript source. Running the command in
 3583: \texttt{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}
 3584: \[
 3585: \texttt{python artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}
 3586: \]
 3587: regenerates the table input from the divisor/rank method of
 3588: Proposition~\ref{prop:effective-birth-layer-computation}.  The printed table is
 3589: therefore an exhaustive enumeration of $A(n)$ and $\#\mathcal M_n$ for
 3590: $2\le n\le30$ using only the finite tests proved above; the displayed
 3591: factorizations of the small integers $F_n$ are produced by the script's exact
 3592: integer trial-division routine. Its complete console output is supplied as
 3593: \path{artifacts/birth_layer_table_output.txt}.
 3594: \input{generated/tab_birth_layer_data.tex}
 3595: 
 3596: \paragraph{Finite verification.}
 3597: The supplementary script
 3598: \texttt{artifacts/verify\_finite\_claims.py} independently compares the actual
 3599: sets obtained by direct divisor/rank enumeration with the upper-fiber and
 3600: witness constructions through $n=60$. It
 3601: then uses the latter formula through $n=210$: inclusion--exclusion computes
 3602: \[
 3603:  A(n)=\sum_{S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}(-1)^{|S|}
 3604:  \tau\!\left(F_{n/\prod_{p\in S}p}\right),
 3605: \]
 3606: and the minimal threshold covers of the conditions
 3607: $q\nmid F_{n/p}$ compute $\mathcal M_n$.  The script checks
 3608: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}, the simplified bound
 3609: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, the odd Bell lower bound, and both finite
 3610: private-cover bounds in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. It also
 3611: computes $R(n)$, checks the pointwise deaggregation bounds
 3612: \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} and
 3613: \eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}, verifies the exact-rank partition
 3614: and \eqref{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole} on every squarefree layer, checks
 3615: the refined upper bound \eqref{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound},
 3616: checks the fibotomic identities \eqref{eq:fibotomic-binet-error} and
 3617: \eqref{eq:exact-rank-radical-divides-fibotomic}, the entropy inequality
 3618: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}, the exact-rank congruence candidate bound,
 3619: and the available cases of \eqref{eq:jarden-exact-rank-consequence}, and
 3620: records the running ratio against the support-entropy scale
 3621: $(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4$. For the rank-pure results, it directly enumerates all
 3622: minimal covers for $k\le4$, checks the Hearne--Wagner and connected-component
 3623: counts, verifies the M\"obius exact-rank-prime formula on every support, and
 3624: tests every canonical rank-pure product against the independently computed
 3625: $\mathcal M_n$. This covers all $208$ layers $3\le n\le210$, including exact
 3626: realization of every minimal cover on all $104$ odd layers. It reports $59/59$
 3627: set equalities for both original comparisons and no finite-bound or rank-pure
 3628: failures. The exact output is
 3629: \path{artifacts/finite_verification.txt}. The exact factorizations used for
 3630: $2\le n\le210$ are recorded in
 3631: \path{artifacts/fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv}; the verifier checks
 3632: every row by multiplication. The Python and SymPy versions, commands, file
 3633: inventory, and verification scope are in \path{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}, and
 3634: \path{artifacts/SHA256SUMS} authenticates the supplied files. These finite
 3635: checks do not provide distributional control of $R(n)$.
 3636: In particular, they do not prove the asymptotic hypotheses \textup{(H2)} or
 3637: \textup{(BW)}.

 3648: Corollary~\ref{cor:four-support-reduction}, the one-, two-, and
 3649: three-coordinate classifications already determine every disconnected
 3650: four-coordinate pattern. Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}

本轮核验确认：关于可见单秩最大值
\[
A^*(n)=\max\left\{1,\max_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)\ne\varnothing}}\#\Pi_\alpha(d)\right\},
\qquad
S(x)=\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
\]
的命题
\[
S(x)=o\!\left(x(\log\log x)^2\right)
\]
仍未得到无条件证明，其临界尺度反向下界亦未得到无条件证明。该未决性及其与平均秩窗稀疏假设的等价接口已经写入本文；不得再次将其表述为新结论，亦不得以启发式、数值样本或条件命题替代无条件承重定理。

文献边界必须作如下校正。Granville 2012 的奇重数定理适用于满足 $c\equiv2\pmod4$ 的一类二阶 Lucas 递推；原文明确指出其方法没有覆盖 $b,c$ 同为奇数的情形，因而不适用于 Fibonacci 递推 $b=c=1$。Hong 2025 证明的是：对每个固定正整数 \(\kappa\)，充分大的 \(n\) 所对应的 \(F_n\) 存在一个不小于 \((\kappa+1)n-1\) 的 primitive divisor；该结论不控制精确秩素数的平均分散或临界集中。Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek 2005 的 \(\omega(F_n)\gg\log n\) 仅为其 Conjecture 5.1；本文已经证明该猜想将推出平均稀疏假设失败的条件性障碍。Stroiński 的累计精确秩素数上界、本文的逐秩 fibotomic 熵不等式及其点态 Euler 因子上界也均已出现。请避开这些已经发表或已在本文证明的论域。

此前关于“fibotomic 熵已经穷尽 primitive part 大小与秩同余所含信息”的最优性断言没有证明。缺失之处是：尚未定义允许使用的信息公理类，也没有构造满足全部公理且达到相应上界的可实现极值模型。本文现仅断言 fibotomic 大小恒等式、精确秩 radical 的整除关系及 $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$ 共同推出既有熵不等式；不得恢复信息穷尽或普适最优性主张，除非同时给出精确公理类与可实现极值模型的完整证明。

下一轮只研究下述单一问题：无条件确定 $S(x)$ 在临界尺度 $x(\log\log x)^2$ 上的真实数量级。必须证明 $S(x)=o(x(\log\log x)^2)$，或证明存在绝对常数 $c>0$ 使
\[
\liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac{S(x)}{x(\log\log x)^2}\ge c,
\]
或给出严格证明表明二者均不成立并确定正确尺度。答案必须包含一个此前未发表、可独立核验的平均分散或临界集中定理，直接作用于可见除数窗中的 \(\#\Pi_\alpha(d)\)；必须逐步证明该定理并明确说明其如何闭合上述三择一判定。不得仅重述 primitive divisor 的存在、单个因子的大小、累计计数上界、逐点 fibotomic 上界或 BLMS 条件推论。

不要重复之前内容.不要重复其他人已经发表公开的推理过程(定理、推论、猜想、命题及证明), 可以使用其他人的结论并引用.不要中间过程结论.请使用学术化语言表达, 禁止口语.

 3250: \]
 3251: on this set. Squarefree integers have density $6/\pi^2$, and squarefree prime
 3252: indices have density zero. Summation over squarefree composite indices proves

 3256: \begin{corollary}[Conditional normal and mean orders]
 3257: \label{cor:conditional-orders}
 3258: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window},
 3259: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-normal-order}
 3260:  L(n)\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
 3261:  \quad\text{for almost all }n.
 3262: \end{equation}
 3263: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window},
 3264: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-mean-order}
 3265:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3266:  \sim\frac{\log2}{4}x(\log\log x)^2.
 3267: \end{equation}
 3268: Neither conclusion is unconditional.
 3269: \end{corollary}
 3270: 
 3271: \begin{proof}
 3272: For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
 3273: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
 3274: Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
 3275: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
 3276: sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
 3277: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
 3278: $\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
 3279: lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
 3280: \end{proof}
 3281: 
 3282: \begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
 3283: \label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
 3284: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
 3285: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the contribution of the
 3286: multiplicity-free minimal-cover model used here: $\log2/4$ is its exact
 3287: quadratic coefficient, the fixed-choice rank-pure sector supplies its
 3288: theta-refined lower terms, and the private-coordinate upper bound retains the
 3289: term $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. The rank partition used in
 3290: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests} applies verbatim to every
 3291: support size: prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
 3292: classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
 3293: Conjectures~\ref{hyp:normal-window} and
 3294: \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for upper distributional control of
 3295: exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.
 3296: 
 3297: The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} proves only the
 3298: consequence obtained by combining the fibotomic size identity with the
 3299: divisibility of the exact-rank radical and the congruences
 3300: $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. Remark
 3301: \ref{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient} shows that even its
 3302: Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either rank-window conjecture. The
 3303: manuscript does not prove that these inputs exhaust the information available
 3304: from fibotomic arithmetic, or that
 3305: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} is optimal within any information class. Such
 3306: an optimality theorem would require, at minimum, a precise axiom class of
 3307: admissible information and a realizable extremal model satisfying those
 3308: axioms. Neither is supplied here. Whether stronger unconditional dispersion or
 3309: concentration consequences follow from fibotomic arithmetic is therefore an
 3310: open interface.
 3311: 
 3312: The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier give
 3313: existence in the Fibonacci setting. Granville proves an odd-multiplicity
 3314: primitive-factor theorem for a class of second-order Lucas recurrences with
 3315: $c\equiv2\pmod4$ and explicitly leaves the odd-$b,c$ case, including the
 3316: Fibonacci recurrence $b=c=1$, outside that theorem
 3317: \cite{Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors}. Thus it is a neighboring
 3318: Lucas-sequence result, not a Fibonacci multiplicity input here. Kiss treats
 3319: related Lucas-sequence primitive-divisor questions
 3320: \cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors}. Hong proves that, for each fixed positive
 3321: integer $\kappa$ and all sufficiently large $n$ with an explicit threshold,
 3322: $F_n$ has a primitive divisor at least $(\kappa+1)n-1$
 3323: \cite{Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}. These results concern existence, parity of
 3324: one primitive factor's multiplicity in the stated Lucas class, or the size of
 3325: one Fibonacci primitive divisor; they do not provide the required average
 3326: dispersion or critical concentration estimates for Fibonacci exact-rank prime
 3327: multiplicities in visible divisor windows. The
 3328: Hardy--Ramanujan and
 3329: Tur\'an theorems control the additive function $\omega(n)$ but not the prime
 3330: factorization of $F_d$ across exact apparition ranks. Likewise, the
 3331: Erd\H{o}s--Wintner theorem \cite{ErdosWintner1939Additive} concerns limiting
 3332: distributions of additive arithmetic functions, whereas $L(n)$ and $R(n)$
 3333: are not additive. No implication from RH or ERH to either rank-window
 3334: conjecture is presently known. Accordingly, the normal and mean equivalents
 3335: \eqref{eq:conditional-normal-order} and
 3336: \eqref{eq:conditional-mean-order} are conditional; the unconditional
 3337: statements are \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} and
 3338: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}, together with the stronger connected lower
 3339: bound \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}. None of these estimates
 3340: implies that the disconnected part of $\mathcal M_n$ is negligible; the
 3341: strictly stronger blockwise hypothesis needed for that conclusion is isolated
 3342: in Theorem~\ref{thm:blockwise-connected-dominance} below.
 3343: \end{remark}
 3344: 
 3345: \begin{lemma}[Refined private-coordinate upper bound]
 3346: \label{lem:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3347: For $m\ge2$, put $s=\omega(m)$ and $r=R(m)$. Then
 3348: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3349:  \#\mathcal M_m\le U_s(r),\qquad
 3350:  U_s(r):=\sum_{t=1}^s\binom st
 3351:              \bigl(r2^{s-t}+1\bigr)^t.
 3352: \end{equation}
 3353: There is an absolute constant $K$ such that, for all $s,r\ge1$,
 3354: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-upper-versus-covers}
 3355:  U_s(r)\le Kr^sC_s.
 3356: \end{equation}
 3357: \end{lemma}
 3358: 
 3359: \begin{proof}
 3360: The case $m=2$ is immediate from $\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$ and $R(2)=1$.
 3361: Suppose $m\ge3$, and let a witness cover have $t$ atoms. Choose the canonical
 3362: private coordinate of each atom as in the proof of
 3363: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth}; let $P$ be the resulting
 3364: $t$-set and $Q$ its complement. For a fixed $x\in P$, a prime atom has full
 3365: support $\{x\}\cup U$ for some $U\subseteq Q$, giving at most
 3366: $r2^{s-t}$ choices. A ladder atom with essential coordinate $x$ must be a
 3367: power of the index prime $x$, and the full-exponent equation in
 3368: Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders} determines at most one such atom
 3369: over all its possible full supports. Thus the atom assigned to $x$ has at most
 3370: $r2^{s-t}+1$ choices. Summing over $P$ and $t$ proves
 3371: \eqref{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}; ignoring coverage and
 3372: coprimeness only enlarges the count.
 3373: 
 3374: For $r\ge1$,
 3375: \[
 3376:  U_s(r)\le r^sU_s(1).
 3377: \]
 3378: If $t\le3s/4$, then, uniformly in this range,
 3379: \[
 3380:  (2^{s-t}+1)^t
 3381:  =2^{t(s-t)}(1+2^{-(s-t)})^t
 3382:  =(1+o(1))2^{t(s-t)}.
 3383: \]
 3384: The sum over $t>3s/4$ is
 3385: $2^{-s^2/16+O(s)}$ times a central term of
 3386: $b_s=\sum_t\binom st2^{t(s-t)}$. Hence $U_s(1)\sim b_s$. The published
 3387: asymptotic \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives $C_s\sim b_s$; enlarging a
 3388: constant to cover the finitely many small values of $s$ proves
 3389: \eqref{eq:refined-upper-versus-covers}.
 3390: \end{proof}
 3391: 
 3392: \begin{theorem}[Blockwise criterion for connected dominance]
 3393: \label{thm:blockwise-connected-dominance}
 3394: Let $(n_j)$ be a sequence, put $V_j=\mathcal P(n_j)$ and
 3395: $k_j=\omega(n_j)\to\infty$, and for nonempty $C\subseteq V_j$ put
 3396: \[
 3397:  (n_j)_C:=\prod_{\ell\in C}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n_j)}.
 3398: \]
 3399: For a nontrivial partition $\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)$ define
 3400: \[
 3401:  \Delta(\Pi):=\sum_{\substack{C,D\in\Pi\\C<D}}|C||D|
 3402:  =\frac{k_j^2-\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|^2}{2}.
 3403: \]
 3404: Suppose
 3405: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blockwise-window-condition}
 3406:  \varepsilon_j:=
 3407:  \max_{\substack{\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)\\|\Pi|\ge2}}
 3408:  \frac{\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)}{\Delta(\Pi)}
 3409:  \longrightarrow0\qquad\text{\rm(BW)}.
 3410: \end{equation}
 3411: Then
 3412: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion}
 3413:  \frac{\#(\mathcal M_{n_j}\setminus\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn})}
 3414:  {\#\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn}}
 3415:  \le\exp\left[-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)k_j\right]
 3416:  =o(1).
 3417: \end{equation}
 3418: A sufficient condition for \eqref{eq:blockwise-window-condition} is
 3419: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blockwise-window-sufficient-condition}
 3420:  \max_{\varnothing\ne C\subsetneq V_j}
 3421:  \frac{\log R((n_j)_C)}{k_j-|C|}\longrightarrow0
 3422:  \qquad\text{\rm(BW$'$)}.
 3423: \end{equation}
 3424: In particular, \eqref{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion} holds if
 3425: $R((n_j)_C)=1$ for every nonempty proper block $C$ and all sufficiently
 3426: large $j$.
 3427: \end{theorem}
 3428: 
 3429: \begin{proof}
 3430: The unique connected-block factorization in
 3431: Theorem~\ref{thm:mingenerator-connected-factorization} gives
 3432: \[
 3433:  \#(\mathcal M_{n_j}\setminus\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn})
 3434:  =\sum_{\substack{\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)\\|\Pi|\ge2}}
 3435:    \prod_{C\in\Pi}M^{\rm conn}((n_j)_C).
 3436: \]
 3437: Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound} to each block.
 3438: The theta asymptotic \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives uniform two-sided
 3439: bounds
 3440: \[
 3441:  C_s\asymp(s+1)^{-1/2}2^{s^2/4+s}.
 3442: \]
 3443: Consequently there is an absolute constant $K_1$ such that every partition
 3444: $\Pi$ with block sizes $s_C=|C|$ contributes, after division by $C_{k_j}$,
 3445: at most
 3446: \begin{equation}\label{eq:partition-contribution-bound}
 3447:  K_1^{|\Pi|}\sqrt{k_j}\,
 3448:  \exp\left\{-\frac{\log2}{2}\Delta(\Pi)
 3449:  +\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)\right\}.
 3450: \end{equation}
 3451: Under \textup{(BW)}, this is
 3452: \[
 3453:  K_1^{|\Pi|}\sqrt{k_j}\,
 3454:  \exp\left\{-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)
 3455:  \Delta(\Pi)\right\},
 3456: \]
 3457: where the $o(1)$ is uniform over all nontrivial partitions.
 3458: 
 3459: If every block has size at most $k_j/2$, then
 3460: $\Delta(\Pi)\ge k_j^2/4$. There are at most $k_j^{k_j}$ set partitions,
 3461: so the total of these terms is $\exp(-\Omega(k_j^2))$. Otherwise there is a
 3462: unique largest block of size $k_j-q>k_j/2$. Then
 3463: $\Delta(\Pi)\ge q(k_j-q)$, and the remaining $q$ vertices can be selected and
 3464: partitioned in at most
 3465: \[
 3466:  \binom{k_j}{q}\mathsf B_q\le(ek_j)^q
 3467: \]
 3468: ways. Summing \eqref{eq:partition-contribution-bound} over
 3469: $1\le q<k_j/2$ therefore gives
 3470: \[
 3471:  \exp\left[-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)k_j\right]C_{k_j}.
 3472: \]
 3473: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} gives
 3474: $M^{\rm conn}(n_j)\ge(1-o(1))C_{k_j}$, proving
 3475: \eqref{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion}.
 3476: 
 3477: Finally, if the maximum in \textup{(BW$'$)} is $\eta_j$, then every block of
 3478: a nontrivial partition is proper and
 3479: \[
 3480:  \sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)
 3481:  \le\eta_j\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|(k_j-|C|)
 3482:  =2\eta_j\Delta(\Pi).
 3483: \]
 3484: Thus \textup{(BW$'$)} implies \textup{(BW)}, and the last assertion is the
 3485: case $\eta_j=0$.
 3486: \end{proof}

 3488: %% ====================================================================
 3489: \section{Numerical evidence}\label{sec:numerical}
 3490: %% ====================================================================
 3491: 
 3492: Table~\ref{tab:birth-layer-data-generated} displays the birth layer cardinality $A(n)$
 3493: and the number of minimal generators $\#\mathcal M_n$ for $2\le n\le 30$,
 3494: computed by exhaustive enumeration. The table is illustrative only: the
 3495: unconditional inequalities in Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} do
 3496: not rely on this finite sample. Several features visible in the
 3497: data agree with the structural results of the preceding sections.
 3498: First, $\#\mathcal M_n=1$ whenever $\omega(n)=1$ and
 3499: $n\notin\{19,25,27\}$; the exceptions arise from composite Fibonacci numbers
 3500: with multiple atomic families at the same prime-power index.
 3501: Second, the jump at $n=12$ ($A(12)=10$, $\#\mathcal M_{12}=3$) and at $n=24$
 3502: ($A(24)=55$, $\#\mathcal M_{24}=4$) reflects the interaction of the
 3503: exceptional prime $2$ with the layers having no exact-rank prime at indices
 3504: $6$ and $12$.
 3505: Third, the first nonempty three-coordinate layer occurs at $n=30$ and has
 3506: $\#\mathcal M_{30}=5$; the displayed witness covers realize exactly the
 3507: types $\Gamma_1$, $\Gamma_4$, $\Gamma_5$, $\Gamma_7$, and $\Gamma_8$.
 3508: The remaining admissible shapes $\Gamma_3$, $\Gamma_6$, and $\Gamma_9$ are
 3509: not realized at $n=30$. This exact five-type assertion, including the three
 3510: nonrealization proofs, is Proposition~\ref{prop:n30-five-type-classification};
 3511: it is not inferred from the table.
 3512: 
 3513: The table was produced by the finite divisor/rank computation in the next
 3514: proposition, applied exhaustively for $2\le n\le30$.  The manuscript source
 3515: includes the short script that performs this enumeration and the generated
 3516: table file input below; these files are supplementary to the mathematical
 3517: criterion rather than part of the theorem stream. For the classical
 3518: factorization literature, see the tables of Brillhart--Montgomery--Silverman
 3519: \cite{BrillhartMontgomerySilverman1988Tables}.
 3520: 
 3521: \begin{proposition}[Finite divisor/rank verification with supplied factorization]
 3522: \label{prop:effective-birth-layer-computation}
 3523: Let $n\ge2$ and suppose the integer factorization of $F_n$ is known. Then
 3524: $B_n$, $A(n)$, and $\mathcal M_n$ are recovered exactly by the following finite
 3525: divisor/rank test.
 3526: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
 3527: \item Enumerate the divisor set $D_n=\operatorname{Div}(F_n)$ from the
 3528:  factorization of $F_n$.
 3529: \item Enumerate the divisor set $\operatorname{Div}(n)$. For each
 3530:  $q\in D_n$, compute
 3531:  \[
 3532:  \alpha_n(q):=\min\{d\mid n:q\mid F_d\}.
 3533:  \]
 3534: \item Set $B_n=\{q\in D_n:q\ge2,\ \alpha_n(q)=n\}$ and
 3535:  $A(n)=\#B_n$.
 3536: \item Sort $B_n$ by divisibility-compatible order, for instance by size, and
 3537:  retain exactly those $m\in B_n$ for which no previously retained element
 3538:  divides $m$; the retained set is $\mathcal M_n$.
 3539: \end{enumerate}
 3540: \end{proposition}
 3541: 
 3542: \begin{proof}
 3543: Since every $q\in D_n$ divides $F_n$, Proposition~\ref{prop:rank-basics}
 3544: shows that $q\mid F_d$ for some $d$ if and only if $\alpha(q)\mid d$. In
 3545: particular $q\mid F_n$ implies $\alpha(q)\mid n$, so the least positive index
 3546: at which $q$ appears is exactly
 3547: \[
 3548: \alpha(q)=\min\{d\mid n:q\mid F_d\}=\alpha_n(q).
 3549: \]
 3550: Hence the test in step (c) gives precisely the definition
 3551: $B_n=\{q\in D_n:\alpha(q)=n\}$, with the harmless exclusion of $q=1$ already
 3552: consistent with the convention $\alpha(1)=1$ and $n\ge2$. Counting this set
 3553: gives $A(n)$.
 3554: 
 3555: It remains to justify step (d). The set $B_n$ is finite. If its elements are
 3556: processed in increasing size, then every proper divisor of the current element
 3557: has already been processed. We claim inductively that every discarded element
 3558: has a retained minimal divisor. The claim is vacuous before the first element.
 3559: If the current element is discarded, it has a previously retained divisor by
 3560: the algorithm, so the claim holds for it; if it is retained, it is its own
 3561: retained divisor. Thus every earlier divisor, retained or discarded, has a
 3562: retained divisor below it. Consequently the current element is retained if and
 3563: only if no smaller element of $B_n$ divides it, which is exactly minimality in
 3564: the divisibility poset. The retained set is therefore $\mathcal M_n$.
 3565: \end{proof}
 3566: 
 3567: \begin{remark}[Reproducibility scope]
 3568: The preceding proposition is used only to document the finite verification
 3569: interface for the table, not as a complexity theorem.  After the factorization
 3570: of $F_n$ has been supplied, the displayed loops involve only the finite divisor
 3571: sets of $F_n$ and $n$, ordinary divisibility tests $q\mid F_d$, and divisor
 3572: comparisons inside $B_n$.  The manuscript makes no bit-complexity assertion and
 3573: does not include the cost of factoring $F_n$ in the numerical evidence.
 3574: \end{remark}
 3575: 
 3576: \paragraph{Table generation.}
 3577: The reproducibility files
 3578: \[
 3579: \text{\texttt{artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}},\qquad
 3580: \text{\texttt{artifacts/tab\_birth\_layer\_data.tex}}
 3581: \]
 3582: are included with the manuscript source. Running the command in
 3583: \texttt{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}
 3584: \[
 3585: \texttt{python artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}
 3586: \]
 3587: regenerates the table input from the divisor/rank method of
 3588: Proposition~\ref{prop:effective-birth-layer-computation}.  The printed table is
 3589: therefore an exhaustive enumeration of $A(n)$ and $\#\mathcal M_n$ for
 3590: $2\le n\le30$ using only the finite tests proved above; the displayed
 3591: factorizations of the small integers $F_n$ are produced by the script's exact
 3592: integer trial-division routine. Its complete console output is supplied as
 3593: \path{artifacts/birth_layer_table_output.txt}.
 3594: \input{generated/tab_birth_layer_data.tex}
 3595: 
 3596: \paragraph{Finite verification.}
 3597: The supplementary script
 3598: \texttt{artifacts/verify\_finite\_claims.py} independently compares the actual
 3599: sets obtained by direct divisor/rank enumeration with the upper-fiber and
 3600: witness constructions through $n=60$. It
 3601: then uses the latter formula through $n=210$: inclusion--exclusion computes
 3602: \[
 3603:  A(n)=\sum_{S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}(-1)^{|S|}
 3604:  \tau\!\left(F_{n/\prod_{p\in S}p}\right),
 3605: \]
 3606: and the minimal threshold covers of the conditions
 3607: $q\nmid F_{n/p}$ compute $\mathcal M_n$.  The script checks
 3608: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}, the simplified bound
 3609: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, the odd Bell lower bound, and both finite
 3610: private-cover bounds in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. It also
 3611: computes $R(n)$, checks the pointwise deaggregation bounds
 3612: \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} and
 3613: \eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}, verifies the exact-rank partition
 3614: and \eqref{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole} on every squarefree layer, checks
 3615: the refined upper bound \eqref{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound},
 3616: checks the fibotomic identities \eqref{eq:fibotomic-binet-error} and
 3617: \eqref{eq:exact-rank-radical-divides-fibotomic}, the entropy inequality
 3618: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}, the exact-rank congruence candidate bound,
 3619: and the available cases of \eqref{eq:jarden-exact-rank-consequence}, and
 3620: records the running ratio against the support-entropy scale
 3621: $(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4$. For the rank-pure results, it directly enumerates all
 3622: minimal covers for $k\le4$, checks the Hearne--Wagner and connected-component
 3623: counts, verifies the M\"obius exact-rank-prime formula on every support, and
 3624: tests every canonical rank-pure product against the independently computed
 3625: $\mathcal M_n$. This covers all $208$ layers $3\le n\le210$, including exact
 3626: realization of every minimal cover on all $104$ odd layers. It reports $59/59$
 3627: set equalities for both original comparisons and no finite-bound or rank-pure
 3628: failures. The exact output is
 3629: \path{artifacts/finite_verification.txt}. The exact factorizations used for
 3630: $2\le n\le210$ are recorded in
 3631: \path{artifacts/fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv}; the verifier checks
 3632: every row by multiplication. The Python and SymPy versions, commands, file
 3633: inventory, and verification scope are in \path{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}, and
 3634: \path{artifacts/SHA256SUMS} authenticates the supplied files. These finite
 3635: checks do not provide distributional control of $R(n)$.
 3636: In particular, they do not prove the asymptotic hypotheses \textup{(H2)} or
 3637: \textup{(BW)}.
 3638: 
 3639: %% ====================================================================
 3640: \section{Further questions}\label{sec:questions}
 3641: %% ====================================================================
 3642: 
 3643: The preceding sections complete the divisor-lattice theorem chain for this
 3644: article.  We record only two boundary markers; neither is used as evidence for
 3645: the results proved above.
 3646: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
 3647: \item Classify connected support kernels of size at least four. By
 3648: Corollary~\ref{cor:four-support-reduction}, the one-, two-, and
 3649: three-coordinate classifications already determine every disconnected
 3650: four-coordinate pattern. Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}
 3651: shows that connected kernels already realize the theta-refined lower bound of
 3652: the fixed-choice rank-pure minimal-cover sector, so a full connected
 3653: high-support classification is not a lower-order residual problem within that
 3654: sector. It would be a separate project.
 3655: \item Prove or disprove either rank-window sparsity conjecture. For the mean
 3656: hypothesis, Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} reduces the
 3657: problem to the visible single-rank maximum
 3658: \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}; Proposition
 3659: \ref{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window} also shows that a published conjecture
 3660: predicts failure rather than sparsity. As explained in
 3661: Remark~\ref{rem:exact-average-open-interface}, the remaining question is an
 3662: upper distribution problem for exact-rank prime multiplicities.
 3663: The paper derives conditional normal and mean orders, but does not
 3664: claim that either conjecture follows from presently known primitive-divisor
 3665: theorems, RH, or ERH.
 3666: \end{enumerate}

 3669: \appendix
 3670: 
 3671: \section{Finite verification for the three-coordinate table}
 3672: \label{app:three-coordinate-checklist}
 3673: 
 3674: \begin{proposition}[Reader's checklist for the three-coordinate table]
 3675: \label{prop:three-coordinate-table-checklist}
 3676: Assume $\mathcal P(n)=\{\ell_1,
 3677: \ell_2,\ell_3\}$.  The following finite procedure returns exactly
 3678: $\mathcal M_n$.
 3679: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
 3680: \item For every nonempty $J\subseteq[3]$, compute the exact-rank window
 3681: $\mathcal R_J^\circ(n)$ and the rank classes
 3682: $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ for $d\in\mathcal R_J^\circ(n)$ by
 3683: Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}. This gives every prime
 3684: slot $P_J(n)$.
 3685: \item For each coordinate $i$, compute the single possible ladder candidate
 3686: $\mathcal L_i^*(n)$ from
 3687: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests}, then retain it in
 3688: $L_{i,J}(n)$ exactly for those $J$ for which its rank divides $n$ and has
 3689: full-coordinate set $J$.
 3690: \item For each coordinate permutation of each of the eight rows in
 3691: Table~\ref{tab:three-coordinate-classification}, replace the displayed slots
 3692: by the finite sets found in (i) and (ii), with
 3693: $S_i(n)=P_{\{i\}}(n)\cup L_{i,\{i\}}(n)$.
 3694: \item Keep precisely those row fillings whose selected prime powers have
 3695: distinct underlying primes, equivalently those satisfying the Hall
 3696: inequalities in Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-slot-selection}; multiply the selected
 3697: prime powers and insert the product into a set, thereby removing repetitions
 3698: caused by stabilizers and coordinate permutations.
 3699: \end{enumerate}
 3700: The products retained in (iv) are exactly the minimal generators listed in
 3701: Theorem~\ref{thm:low-support-classification-package}.  In particular, the
 3702: table is a closed finite classification for fixed $n$, not an interface to an
 3703: additional infinite search.
 3704: \end{proposition}

# Literature and Novelty Check

Checked 2026-08-02. The searches below used the public arXiv Atom API
(`export.arxiv.org/api/query`), not a secondary search index. DOI metadata was
cross-checked against the Crossref REST API (`api.crossref.org/works`). An
absence from these searches is evidence about the queried corpus, not a proof
of global priority.

## arXiv API Queries

The API was queried for `"rank of apparition"`, `"order of appearance" AND
Fibonacci`, `"primitive divisor" AND Lucas`, `Erdos-Wintner`, `"average
order" AND Fibonacci`, `"witness cover" AND Fibonacci`, and `Fibonacci AND
apparition AND fiber`, with up to 100 records per query. A subsequent `id_list`
query confirmed the metadata of the specific records cited below.

- Rank/order of appearance: arXiv:2309.14501, *Dynamics of the Fibonacci
  Order of Appearance Map* (published DOI
  `10.1080/00150517.2025.2515497`); arXiv:1606.01715, Stroiński, *On
  Dirichlet Products Evaluated at Fibonacci Numbers*; arXiv:1511.09038,
  Silverman, *Divisor Divisibility Sequences on Tori*. These concern dynamics,
  contraction/counting identities, or general divisibility sequences. None
  states an order theorem for the minimal elements of an apparition fiber.
- Primitive divisors: arXiv:1201.6659 (DOI
  `10.1090/S0025-5718-1995-1284673-6`), arXiv:1211.3107 (DOI
  `10.5802/jtnb.168`), and arXiv:1211.3108 (DOI
  `10.1017/S0305004197002223`) are Voutier's papers on primitive divisors of
  Lucas and Lehmer sequences. The definitive uniform theorem used here is
  Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier, DOI `10.1515/crll.2001.080`; the API returned no
  exact arXiv record for that article.
- Erdős--Wintner: arXiv:2009.05435, *Effective Erdős-Wintner theorems for
  digital expansions*, and arXiv:2512.20882, *Erdős-Wintner theorem for
  linear recurrent bases*. These are digital/numeration analogues, not
  results on prime-factor multiplicities of Fibonacci numbers.
- Novelty phrases: the API returned no entries for `"witness cover" AND
  Fibonacci` or for `Fibonacci AND apparition AND fiber`. It also returned no
  paper giving an asymptotic, normal order, or limiting law for the present
  quantity `#M_n`.

## Exact Classical Citations

- Wall, *Fibonacci Series Modulo m*, DOI
  `10.1080/00029890.1960.11989541`; Vinson, *The Relation of the Period Modulo
  m to the Rank of Apparition of m in the Fibonacci Sequence*, DOI
  `10.1080/00150517.1963.12431578`.
- Carmichael, *On the Numerical Factors of the Arithmetic Forms
  alpha^n +/- beta^n*, DOI `10.2307/1967797` and continuation DOI
  `10.2307/1967798`; Yabuta, *A Simple Proof of Carmichael's Theorem on
  Primitive Divisors*, DOI `10.1080/00150517.2001.12428701`;
  Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier, DOI `10.1515/crll.2001.080`.
- Fulton--Morris, *On Arithmetical Functions Related to the Fibonacci
  Numbers*, DOI `10.4064/aa-16-2-105-110`; Lengyel, *The Order of the
  Fibonacci and Lucas Numbers*, DOI `10.1080/00150517.1995.12429139`.
- Luca--Pomerance, *On the Local Behavior of the Order of Appearance in the
  Fibonacci Sequence*, DOI `10.1142/S1793042114500079`; Cameron et al.,
  *On the Properties of Fibotomic Polynomials*, DOI
  `10.1016/j.aam.2022.102344`.
- Hardy--Ramanujan, *The Normal Number of Prime Factors of a Number n*,
  *Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics* 48 (1917), 76--92
  (no DOI located); Turán, *On a Theorem of Hardy and Ramanujan*, DOI
  `10.1112/jlms/s1-9.4.274`.
- Erdős--Wintner, *Additive Arithmetical Functions and Statistical
  Independence*, DOI `10.2307/2371326`. The theorem concerns limiting
  distributions of additive functions. The function `log #M_n` is not
  additive, so Erdős--Wintner cannot close the present interface without a
  separate theorem controlling primitive-rank windows.

## Novelty Boundary and Residual Interface

The new order statement is combinatorial-arithmetic: after grouping atomic
prime powers by their essential/full support pair, the witness-cover private
coordinates give the exact entropy constant `log(2)/4`. Unconditionally,

`log #M_n >= (log(2)/4) omega(n)^2 + O(1)` as `omega(n) -> infinity`,

and the matching upper bound differs only by
`omega(n) log R(n)`, where `R(n)` is the maximum cardinality of one atomic
rank window. Consequently the paper proves the exact normal and mean orders
under explicitly stated normal-density and mean rank-window sparsity
hypotheses. No located paper contains this witness-cover entropy theorem.

The remaining arithmetic statement, `log R(n)=o(omega(n))` for almost all
`n` (or its mean analogue), is not a consequence of Carmichael/BHV, the
prime-power lifting formulas, Hardy--Ramanujan, Turán, Erdős--Wintner, RH, or
ERH as presently known. Carmichael/BHV supplies at least one primitive prime
in each nonexceptional rank; it gives no upper distribution law for the number
of primitive primes in the rank windows. The manuscript therefore labels
this precise multiplicity assertion as an open interface and does not present
it as proved.

## Follow-up Check for the Fibotomic Rank-Entropy Bound

Checked 2026-08-03. The arXiv Atom API was queried for fibotomic, rank of
apparition AND Fibonacci, order of appearance AND Fibonacci, primitive prime
divisors AND Fibonacci, number of primitive divisors AND Fibonacci, prime
factors AND fibotomic, and primitive part AND Fibonacci. Crossref and OpenAlex
were queried independently for the corresponding bibliographic phrases. No
returned record states the pointwise estimate
\[
 a(d)\leq\left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log d},
\]
the fibotomic entropy inequality from which it follows, the induced bound for
the visible maximum \(A^*(n)\), or either weighted estimate for
\(\sum\omega(n)\log A^*(n)\).

The full texts of arXiv:2009.03345 and arXiv:1606.01715 were checked.
Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong prove the fibotomic
polynomial factorization used as an input, but do not derive an exact-rank
prime-count entropy bound. Stroiński's Theorem 7 proves only the already-cited
cumulative estimate
\[
 \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 \#\{p:\alpha(p)\leq x\}
 \leq\frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
\]
That cumulative conclusion remains attributed to Stroiński and is not claimed
as a new theorem here.

Jarden's original one-page article was retrieved from the Fibonacci Quarterly
archive and read in full. Its exact metadata are D. Jarden, *Any Lucas Number
\(L_{5p}\), for Any Prime \(p>5\), Has at Least Two Distinct Primitive Prime
Divisors*, *The Fibonacci Quarterly* 6 (1968), no. 6, 407, DOI
10.1080/00150517.1968.12431197. The paper supplies the two primitive Lucas
prime divisors; the present manuscript separately proves that each has
Fibonacci rank \(10p\) before using it in the weighted lower bound.

The absence of a match in these searches is not a proof of global priority.
It is the stated evidence for the novelty classification used in this
revision.

    <summary>Let $(F_n)_{n \geq 1}$ be the sequence of Fibonacci numbers. For all integers $a$ and $b \geq 1$ with $\gcd(a, b) = 1$, let $[a^{-1} \!\bmod b]$ be the multiplicative inverse of $a$ modulo $b$, which we pick in the usual set of representatives $\{0, 1, \dots, b-1\}$.     <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/2212.06127v1</id>
    <title>On the index of appearance of a Lucas sequence</title>
    <updated>2022-12-12T18:55:55Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.06127v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.06127v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Let $\mathbf{u} = (u_n)_{n \geq 0}$ be a Lucas sequence, that is, a sequence of integers satisfying $u_0 = 0$, $u_1 = 1$, and $u_n = a_1 u_{n - 1} + a_2 u_{n - 2}$ for every integer $n \geq 2$, where $a_1$ and $a_2$ are fixed nonzero integers. For each prime number $p$ with $p \nmid 2a_2D_{\mathbf{u}}$, where $D_{\mathbf{u}} := a_1^2 + 4a_2$, let $ρ_{\mathbf{u}}(p)$ be the rank of appearance of $p$ in $\mathbf{u}$, that is, the smallest positive integer $k$ such that $p \mid u_k$. It is well known that $ρ_{\mathbf{u}}(p)$ exists and that $p \equiv \big(D_{\mathbf{u}} \mid p \big) \pmod {ρ_{\mathbf{u}}(p)}$, where $\big(D_{\mathbf{u}} \mid p \big)$ is the Legendre symbol. Define the index of appearance of $p$ in $\mathbf{u}$ as $ι_{\mathbf{u}}(p) := \left(p - \big(D_{\mathbf{u}} \mid p \big)\right) / ρ_{\mathbf{u}}(p)$. For each positive integer $t$ and for every $x &gt; 0$, let $\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{u}}(t, x)$ be the set of prime numbers $p$ such that $p \leq x$, $p \nmid 2a_2 D_{\mathbf{u}}$, and $ι_{\mathbf{u}}(p) = t$. Under the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, and under some mild assumptions on $\mathbf{u}$, we prove that \begin{equation*}
  \#\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{u}}(t, x) = A\, F_{\mathbf{u}}(t) \, G_{\mathbf{u}}(t) \, \frac{x}{\log x} + O_{\mathbf{u}}\!\left(\frac{x}{(\log x)^2} + \frac{x \log (2\log x)}{\varphi(t) (\log x)^2}\right) , \end{equation*} for all positive integers $t$ and for all $x &gt; t^3$, where $A$ is the Artin constant, $F_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot)$ is a multiplicative function, and $G_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot)$ is a periodic function (both these functions are effectively computable in terms of $\mathbf{u}$). Furthermore, we provide some explicit examples and numerical data.</summary>

    <summary>MinRank is an NP-complete problem in linear algebra whose characteristics make it attractive to build post-quantum cryptographic primitives. Several MinRank-based digital signature schemes have been proposed. In particular, two of them, MIRA and MiRitH, have been submitted to the NIST Post-Quantum Cryptography Standardization Process.     <title>On the p-adic denseness of the quotient set of a polynomial image</title>
    <updated>2018-07-31T16:57:31Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.08947v2" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1807.08947v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>The quotient set, or ratio set, of a set of integers $A$ is defined as $R(A) := \left\{a/b : a,b \in A,\; b \neq 0\right\}$. We consider the case in which $A$ is the image of $\mathbb{Z}^+$ under a polynomial $f \in \mathbb{Z}[X]$, and we give some conditions under which $R(A)$ is dense in $\mathbb{Q}_p$. Then, we apply these results to determine when $R(S_m^n)$ is dense in $\mathbb{Q}_p$, where $S_m^n$ is the set of numbers of the form $x_1^n + \cdots + x_m^n$, with $x_1, \dots, x_m \geq 0$ integers. This allows us to answer a question posed in [Garcia et al., $p$-adic quotient sets, Acta Arith. 179, 163-184]. We end leaving an open question.</summary>

    <title>On the least common multiple of random $q$-integers</title>

    <summary>For every positive integer $n$ and for every $α\in [0, 1]$, let $\mathcal{B}(n, α)$ denote the probabilistic model in which a random set $\mathcal{A} \subseteq \{1, \dots, n\}$ is constructed by picking independently each element of $\{1, \dots, n\}$ with probability $α$. Cilleruelo, Rué, Šarka, and Zumalacárregui proved an almost sure asymptotic formula for the logarithm of the least common multiple of the elements of $\mathcal{A}$.

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15157v1</id>
    <title>Another factor of integer polynomials with minimal integrals</title>
    <updated>2026-04-16T15:36:32Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15157v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.15157v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Let $N$ be a positive integer and let $S_N$ be the set of polynomials with integer coefficients, degree less than $N$, and minimal positive integral over $[0,1]$. D. Bazzanella initiated the study of $S_N$ because of its relation to the distribution of prime numbers. Indeed, it is possible to prove that $\sum_{p^m \leq N} \log p = -\log \int_0^1 P(x) \mathrm{d} x$ for every $P \in S_N$, where the sum runs over prime numbers $p$ and positive integers $m$ such that $p^m \leq N$. For each real number $t$, let $\lfloor t \rfloor$ denote the maximal integer not exceeding $t$. The main result of this paper states that there exist infinitely many polynomials $P \in S_N$ such that $\big(x^3(1 - x)^2\big)^{\lfloor N / 6 \rfloor}$ divides $P(x)$ in $\mathbb{Z}[x]$. This improves upon a similar result of Sanna, who proved the same claim but with the lower-degree polynomial $\big(x(1-x)\big)^{\lfloor N / 3 \rfloor}$ in place of $\big(x^3(1 - x)^2\big)^{\lfloor N / 6 \rfloor}$.</summary>

    <title>Practical central binomial coefficients</title>
    <updated>2020-04-11T11:52:10Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.05376v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2004.05376v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>A practical number is a positive integer $n$ such that all positive integers less than $n$ can be written as a sum of distinct divisors of $n$. Leonetti and Sanna proved that, as $x \to +\infty$, the central binomial coefficient $\binom{2n}{n}$ is a practical number for all positive integers $n \leq x$ but at most $O(x^{0.88097})$ exceptions.     <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1907.11877v1</id>
    <title>Directions sets: A generalization of ratio sets</title>
    <updated>2019-07-27T09:04:11Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.11877v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.11877v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For every integer $k \geq 2$ and every $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$, we define the \emph{$k$-directions sets} of $A$ as $D^k(A) := \{{\bf a} / \|{\bf a}\| : {\bf a} \in A^k\}$ and $D^{\underline{k}}(A) := \{{\bf a} / \|{\bf a}\| : {\bf a} \in A^{\underline{k}}\}$, where $\|\cdot\|$ is the Euclidean norm and $A^{\underline{k}} := \{{\bf a} \in A^k : a_i \neq a_j \text{ for all } i \neq j\}$. Via an appropriate homeomorphism, $D^k(A)$ is a generalization of the \emph{ratio set} $R(A) := \{a / b : a,b \in A\}$, which has been studied by many authors. We study $D^k(A)$ and $D^{\underline{k}}(A)$ as subspaces of $S^{k-1} := \{{\bf x} \in [0,1]^k : \|{\bf x}\| = 1\}$. In~particular, generalizing a result of Bukor and Tóth, we provide a characterization of the sets $X \subseteq S^{k-1}$ such that there exists $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ satisfying $D^{\underline{k}}(A)^\prime = X$, where $Y^\prime$ denotes the set of accumulation points of $Y$. Moreover, we provide a simple sufficient condition for $D^k(A)$ to be dense in $S^{k-1}$. We conclude leaving some questions for further research.</summary>

    <title>A note on the natural density of product sets</title>
    <updated>2020-06-23T22:04:13Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.13356v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2006.13356v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Given two sets of natural numbers $\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{B}$ of natural density $1$ we prove that their product set $\mathcal{A}\cdot \mathcal{B}:=\{ab:a\in\mathcal{A},\,b\in\mathcal{B}\}$ also has natural density $1$.     <summary>We prove that the Ring Learning With Errors (RLWE) and the Polynomial Learning With Errors (PLWE) problems over the cyclotomic field $\mathbb{Q}(ζ_n)$ are not equivalent.     <title>Greatest common divisors of shifted primes and Fibonacci numbers</title>

    <summary>Let $(F_n)$ be the sequence of Fibonacci numbers and, for each positive integer $k$, let $\mathcal{P}_k$ be the set of primes $p$ such that $\gcd(p - 1, F_{p - 1}) = k$. We prove that the relative density $\text{r}(\mathcal{P}_k)$ of $\mathcal{P}_k$ exists, and we give a formula for $\text{r}(\mathcal{P}_k)$ in terms of an absolutely convergent series.     <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1808.00374v1</id>
    <title>$p$-adic denseness of members of partitions of $\mathbb{N}$ and their ratio sets</title>
    <updated>2018-08-01T15:29:06Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1808.00374v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1808.00374v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>The ratio set of a set of positive integers $A$ is defined as $R(A) := \{a / b : a, b \in A\}$. The study of the denseness of $R(A)$ in the set of positive real numbers is a classical topic and, more recently, the denseness in the set of $p$-adic numbers $\mathbb{Q}_p$ has also been investigated. Let $A_1, \ldots, A_k$ be a partition of $\mathbb{N}$ into $k$ sets. We prove that for all prime numbers $p$ but at most $\lfloor \log_2 k \rfloor$ exceptions at least one of $R(A_1), \ldots, R(A_k)$ is dense in $\mathbb{Q}_p$. Moreover, we show that for all prime numbers $p$ but at most $k - 1$ exceptions at least one of $A_1, \ldots, A_k$ is dense in $\mathbb{Z}_p$. Both these results are optimal in the sense that there exist partitions $A_1, \ldots, A_k$ having exactly $\lfloor \log_2 k \rfloor$, respectively $k - 1$, exceptional prime numbers; and we give explicit constructions for them. Furthermore, as a corollary, we answer negatively a question raised by Garcia et al.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/2403.14812v2</id>
    <title>Counting numbers that are divisible by the product of their digits</title>
    <updated>2024-04-03T15:30:06Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.14812v2" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.14812v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Let $b \geq 3$ be a positive integer. A natural number is said to be a base-$b$ Zuckerman number if it is divisible by the product of its base-$b$ digits. Let $\mathcal{Z}_b(x)$ be the set of base-$b$ Zuckerman numbers that do not exceed $x$, and assume that $x \to +\infty$. First, we prove an upper bound of the form $|\mathcal{Z}_b(x)| &lt; x^{z_b^{+} + o(1)}$, where $z_b^{+} \in (0,1)$ is an effectively computable constant. In particular, we have that $z_{10}^+ = 0.665{\scriptstyle\ldots}$, which improves upon the previous upper bound $|\mathcal{Z}_{10}(x)| &lt; x^{0.717}$ due to Sanna. Moreover, we prove that $|\mathcal{Z}_{10}(x)| &gt; x^{0.204}$, which improves upon the previous lower bound $|\mathcal{Z}_{10}(x)| &gt; x^{0.122}$, due to De Koninck and Luca. Second, we provide a heuristic suggesting that $|\mathcal{Z}_b(x)| = x^{z_b + o(1)}$, where $z_b \in (0,1)$ is an effectively computable constant. In particular, we have that $z_{10} = 0.419{\scriptstyle\ldots}$. Third, we provide algorithms to count, respectively enumerate, the elements of $\mathcal{Z}_b(x)$, and we determine their complexities. Implementing one of such counting algorithms, we computed $|\mathcal{Z}_b(x)|$ for $b=3,\dots,12$ and large values of $x$ (depending on $b$), and we showed that the results are consistent with our heuristic.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1705.01805v2</id>
    <title>The density of numbers $n$ having a prescribed G.C.D. with the $n$th Fibonacci number</title>
    <updated>2018-03-12T20:05:12Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.01805v2" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1705.01805v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For each positive integer $k$, let $\mathscr{A}_k$ be the set of all positive integers $n$ such that $\gcd(n, F_n) = k$, where $F_n$ denotes the $n$th Fibonacci number. We prove that the asymptotic density of $\mathscr{A}_k$ exists and is equal to $$\sum_{d = 1}^\infty \frac{μ(d)}{\operatorname{lcm}(dk, z(dk))}$$ where $μ$ is the Möbius function and $z(m)$ denotes the least positive integer $n$ such that $m$ divides $F_n$. We also give an effective criterion to establish when the asymptotic density of $\mathscr{A}_k$ is zero and we show that this is the case if and only if $\mathscr{A}_k$ is empty.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1507.08091v1</id>
    <title>On the closure of the image of the generalized divisor function</title>
    <updated>2015-07-29T10:17:49Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1507.08091v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1507.08091v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For any real number $s$, let $σ_s$ be the generalized divisor function, i.e., the arithmetic function defined by $σ_s(n) := \sum_{d \, \mid \, n} d^s$, for all positive integers $n$. We prove that for any $r &gt; 1$ the topological closure of $σ_{-r}(\mathbf{N}^+)$ is the union of a finite number of pairwise disjoint closed intervals $I_1, \ldots, I_\ell$. Moreover, for $k=1,\ldots,\ell$, we show that the set of positive integers $n$ such that $σ_{-r}(n) \in I_k$ has a positive rational asymptotic density $d_k$. In fact, we provide a method to give exact closed form expressions for $I_1, \ldots, I_\ell$ and $d_1, \ldots, d_\ell$, assuming to know $r$ with sufficient precision. As an example, we show that for $r = 2$ it results $\ell = 3$, $I_1 = [1, π^2/9]$, $I_2 = [10/9, π^2/8]$, $I_3 = [5/4, π^2 / 6]$, $d_1 = 1/3$, $d_2 = 1/6$, and $d_3 = 1/2$.</summary>

  <entry>
    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1703.10047v1</id>
    <title>Distribution of integral values for the ratio of two linear recurrences</title>
    <updated>2017-03-29T14:07:39Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1703.10047v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1703.10047v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Let $F$ and $G$ be linear recurrences over a number field $\mathbb{K}$, and let $\mathfrak{R}$ be a finitely generated subring of $\mathbb{K}$. Furthermore, let $\mathcal{N}$ be the set of positive integers $n$ such that $G(n) \neq 0$ and $F(n) / G(n) \in \mathfrak{R}$. Under mild hypothesis, Corvaja and Zannier proved that $\mathcal{N}$ has zero asymptotic density. We prove that $\#(\mathcal{N} \cap [1, x]) \ll x \cdot (\log\log x / \log x)^h$ for all $x \geq 3$, where $h$ is a positive integer that can be computed in terms of $F$ and $G$. Assuming the Hardy-Littlewood $k$-tuple conjecture, our result is optimal except for the term $\log \log x$.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/2401.07661v1</id>
    <title>On the number of residues of certain second-order linear recurrences</title>
    <updated>2024-01-15T13:04:06Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.07661v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.07661v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For every monic polynomial $f \in \mathbb{Z}[X]$ with $\operatorname{deg}(f) \geq 1$, let $\mathcal{L}(f)$ be the set of all linear recurrences with values in $\mathbb{Z}$ and characteristic polynomial $f$, and let \begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{R}(f) := \big\{ρ(\mathbf{x}; m) : \mathbf{x} \in \mathcal{L}(f), \, m \in \mathbb{Z}^+ \big\} , \end{equation*} where $ρ(\mathbf{x}; m)$ is the number of distinct residues of $\mathbf{x}$ modulo $m$.
  Dubickas and Novikas proved that $\mathcal{R}(X^2 - X - 1) = \mathbb{Z}^+$. We generalize this result by showing that $\mathcal{R}(X^2 - a_1 X - 1) = \mathbb{Z}^+$ for every nonzero integer $a_1$. As a corollary, we deduce that for all integers $a_1 \geq 1$ and $k \geq 4$ there exists $ξ\in \mathbb{R}$ such that the sequence of fractional parts $\big(\!\operatorname{frac}(ξα^n)\big)_{n \geq 0}$, where $α:= \big(a_1 + \sqrt{a_1^2 + 4}\,\big) / 2$, has exactly $k$ limit points. Our proofs are constructive and employ some results on the existence of special primitive divisors of certain Lehmer sequences.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1909.04342v1</id>
    <title>On the l.c.m. of random terms of binary recurrence sequences</title>
    <updated>2019-09-10T08:02:42Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1909.04342v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1909.04342v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For every positive integer $n$ and every $δ\in [0,1]$, let $B(n, δ)$ denote the probabilistic model in which a random set $A \subseteq \{1, \dots, n\}$ is constructed by choosing independently every element of $\{1, \dots, n\}$ with probability $δ$. Moreover, let $(u_k)_{k \geq 0}$ be an integer sequence satisfying $u_k = a_1 u_{k - 1} + a_2 u_{k - 2}$, for every integer $k \geq 2$, where $u_0 = 0$, $u_1 \neq 0$, and $a_1, a_2$ are fixed nonzero integers; and let $α$ and $β$, with $|α| \geq |β|$, be the two roots of the polynomial $X^2 - a_1 X - a_2$. Also, assume that $α/ β$ is not a root of unity. We prove that, as $δn / \log n \to +\infty$, for every $A$ in $B(n, δ)$ we have $$\log \operatorname{lcm} (u_a : a \in A) \sim \frac{δ\operatorname{Li}_2(1 - δ)}{1 - δ} \cdot \frac{3\log\!\big|α/ \!\sqrt{(a_1^2, a_2)}\big|}{π^2} \cdot n^2 $$ with probability $1 - o(1)$, where $\operatorname{lcm}$ denotes the lowest common multiple, $\operatorname{Li}_2$ is the dilogarithm, and the factor involving $δ$ is meant to be equal to $1$ when $δ= 1$. This extends previous results of Akiyama, Tropak, Matiyasevich, Guy, Kiss and Mátyás, who studied the deterministic case $δ= 1$, and is motivated by an asymptotic formula for $\operatorname{lcm}(A)$ due to Cilleruelo, Rué, Šarka, and Zumalacárregui.</summary>

    <title>On the least common multiple of shifted powers</title>
    <updated>2021-03-14T16:32:50Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.07967v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2103.07967v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Let $a \geq 2$ be an integer. We prove that for every periodic sequence $(s_n)_{n \geq 1}$ in $\{-1, +1\}$ there exists an effectively computable rational number $C_\mathbf{s} &gt; 0$ such that \begin{equation*} \log\operatorname{lcm}(a + s_1, a^2 + s_2, \dots, a^n + s_n) \sim C_\mathbf{s} \cdot \frac{\log a}{π^2} \cdot n^2 , \end{equation*} as $n \to +\infty$, where $\operatorname{lcm}$ denotes the least common multiple.

    <title>On the p-adic valuation of Stirling numbers of the first kind</title>
    <updated>2016-06-04T14:03:49Z</updated>

    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1605.07424v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For all integers $n \geq k \geq 1$, define $H(n,k) := \sum 1 / (i_1 \cdots i_k)$, where the sum is extended over all positive integers $i_1 &lt; \cdots &lt; i_k \leq n$. Motivated by the works of Erdős-Niven and Chen-Tang, we study the $p$-adic valuation of $H(n,k)$. In particular, for any prime number $p$, integer $k \geq 2$, and $x \geq (k-1)p$, we prove that $ν_p(H(n,k)) &lt; -(k - 1)(\log_p(n/(k - 1)) - 1)$ for all positive integers $n \in [(k-1)p, x]$ whose base $p$ representations start with the base $p$ representation of $k - 1$, but at most $3x^{0.835}$ exceptions. We also generalize a result of Lengyel by giving a description of $ν_2(H(n,2))$ in terms of an infinite binary sequence.</summary>

    <summary>Let $\mathcal{A}$ be the set of all integers of the form $\gcd(n, F_n)$, where $n$ is a positive integer and $F_n$ denotes the $n$th Fibonacci number. We prove that $\#\left(\mathcal{A} \cap [1, x]\right) \gg x / \log x$ for all $x \geq 2$, and that $\mathcal{A}$ has zero asymptotic density. Our proofs rely on a recent result of Cubre and Rouse which gives, for each positive integer $n$, an explicit formula for the density of primes $p$ such that $n$ divides the rank of appearance of $p$, that is, the smallest positive integer $k$ such that $p$ divides $F_k$.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05058v1</id>
    <title>A note on primes in certain residue classes</title>
    <updated>2017-10-13T19:27:33Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05058v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1710.05058v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Given positive integers $a_1,\ldots,a_k$, we prove that the set of primes $p$ such that $p \not\equiv 1 \bmod{a_i}$ for $i=1,\ldots,k$ admits asymptotic density relative to the set of all primes which is at least $\prod_{i=1}^k \left(1-\frac{1}{\varphi(a_i)}\right)$, where $\varphi$ is the Euler's totient function. This result is similar to the one of Heilbronn and Rohrbach, which says that the set of positive integer $n$ such that $n \not\equiv 0 \bmod a_i$ for $i=1,\ldots,k$ admits asymptotic density which is at least $\prod_{i=1}^k \left(1-\frac{1}{a_i}\right)$.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1603.09310v3</id>
    <title>On the k-regularity of the k-adic valuation of Lucas sequences</title>
    <updated>2016-06-04T13:59:57Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.09310v3" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1603.09310v3" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For integers $k \geq 2$ and $n \neq 0$, let $v_k(n)$ denotes the greatest nonnegative integer $e$ such that $k^e$ divides $n$. Moreover, let $u_n$ be a nondegenerate Lucas sequence satisfying $u_0 = 0$, $u_1 = 1$, and $u_{n + 2} = a u_{n + 1} + b u_n$, for some integers $a$ and $b$. Shu and Yao showed that for any prime number $p$ the sequence $v_p(u_{n + 1})$ is $p$-regular, while Medina and Rowland found the rank of $v_p(F_{n + 1})$, where $F_n$ is the $n$-th Fibonacci number. We prove that if $k$ and $b$ are relatively prime then $v_k(u_{n + 1})$ is a $k$-regular sequence, and for $k$ a prime number we also determine its rank. Furthermore, as an intermediate result, we give explicit formulas for $v_k(u_n)$, generalizing a previous theorem of Sanna concerning $p$-adic valuations of Lucas sequences.</summary>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/2309.12724v2</id>
    <title>A note on the power sums of the number of Fibonacci partitions</title>
    <updated>2025-05-03T11:59:34Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.12724v2" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.12724v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For every nonnegative integer $n$, let $r_F(n)$ be the number of ways to write $n$ as a sum of Fibonacci numbers, where the order of the summands does not matter. Moreover, for all positive integers $p$ and $N$, let \begin{equation*} S_{F}^{(p)}(N) := \sum_{n = 0}^{N - 1} \big(r_F(n)\big)^p . \end{equation*} Chow, Jones, and Slattery determined the order of growth of $S_{F}^{(p)}(N)$ for $p \in \{1,2\}$. We prove that, for all positive integers $p$, there exists a real number $λ_p &gt; 1$ such that \begin{equation*} S^{(p)}_F(N) \asymp_p N^{(\log λ_p) /\!\log \varphi} \end{equation*} as $N \to +\infty$, where $\varphi := (1 + \sqrt{5})/2$ is the golden ratio. Furthermore, we show that \begin{equation*} \lim_{p \to +\infty} λ_p^{1/p} = \varphi^{1/2} . \end{equation*} Our proofs employ automata theory and a result on the generalized spectral radius due to Blondel and Nesterov.</summary>
